\subsection{From applied field to a squared-envelope beat component}\label{sec:coupling}
We \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{use the quasistatic electromagnetic approximation for the applied }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{neglect propagation and induction in the extracellular }\DIFaddend field $E(t)$ (V/m),
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{neglecting propagation and induction. This does not make membrane charginginstantaneous. Let }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{but retain finite membrane charging. The effective polarization }\DIFaddend $s(t)$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{be the polarization }\DIFdelend (mV)
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{after the cellular access response;
the static population model reads
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{enters the population sigmoid after cellular access:
}\DIFaddend \begin{equation}
r(t)=\Sig\!\big(v(t)+s(t)\big),\qquad
E(t)=E_1\cos\omega_1 t+E_2\cos\omega_2 t .
\label{eq:coupling}
\end{equation}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The field acts directly on soma}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Soma}\DIFaddend , dendrites, AIS, nodes, and terminals \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{,
each with
its own coupling and frequency response . In a reduced description, their
contributions to the effective input are represented by parallel entry points.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{contribute parallel inputs with
different couplings and response times. }\DIFaddend For nearby carriers centered at
$\omega_c=2\pi f_c$,
\begin{equation}
s(t)\simeq\sum_k \boldsymbol\lambda^k_f(\omega_c)\!\cdot\!\mathbf E(t),\qquad
\boldsymbol\lambda^k_f(\omega)=\boldsymbol\lambda^k_0\,\lvert H_k(\omega)\rvert,
\label{eq:doors}
\end{equation}
where $\boldsymbol\lambda^k_0$ is \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{compartment $k$'s }\DIFaddend static polarization vector
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{of compartment $k$ }\DIFdelend (a length) \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{,
}\DIFdelend and $H_k$ its access filter \cite{ruffini2025rosetta,galangadea2023}.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Equation~\eqref{eq:doors} uses amplitude
gains and }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{This amplitude-gain sum }\DIFaddend assumes aligned compartment phases; \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{a general sum requires }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{otherwise }\DIFaddend the
complex transfers \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, including their relative phases. The passive compartment equations in
App.~\ref{app:access} give both factors from the same voltage response.
The static gain and its frequency dependence belong to this
one response. A model driven by post-coupling polarization and a model driven by
current through its own membrane equation must therefore count that filtering once. }%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{For a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{must be summed. For one }\DIFaddend dominant entry point,
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{define $\lambda_f(\omega)=\lambda_0|H(\omega)|$.
Then }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\lambda_f=\lambda_0|H|$ and }\DIFaddend $\varepsilon_j=\lambda_f(\omega_j)E_j$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{denotes the
polarization reaching the nonlinearity.
The direct-coupling calculation takes
$\lambda_f\approx\lambda_0$ to isolate mixing and recurrent amplification;
\S\ref{sec:khz} restores frequency-dependent access.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{.
Holding these post-access polarizations fixed isolates detection and gain;
at fixed field they inherit $H$'s frequency dependence.
}

\DIFaddend Fast axonal access motivates \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{this limit; compartment anatomy enters the reduced model through
the coupling and response time, which must be calibrated for the preparation. It also fixes the projection direction. A laminar direction for a pyramidal
axon initial segment,
a local axon direction for a node, or an orientation
distribution for terminals defines a different access model. Isotropic averaging
is one possible distribution, not an anatomical consequence of choosing terminals.
Maximizing a geometric map over orientation gives an upper bound for that map
at each location; it does not identify the detector's orientation. The FEM
comparison in \S\ref{sec:fem-methods} therefore holds its chosen direction fixed
when changing the current ratio}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\lambda_f\approx\lambda_0$. At the AIS,
$\Sig(v+s)$ represents a spike-initiation threshold shifted from $v_0$ to
$v_0-s$, requiring no backward transmission to the soma. The conversion from
AIS polarization to $s$ and its response time require calibration. A static
detector additionally requires relaxation faster than the carrier}\DIFaddend .

Expanding \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Sig$ about a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{about the }\DIFaddend field-free \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{operating }\DIFdelend voltage $v^*$ (mV)
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(a Volterra/Taylor expansion, as
in \cite{ruffini2025ham} ) gives
$\Sig(v^*+s)\approx\Sig(v^*)+\Sig'(v^*)\,s+\half\Sig''(v^*)\,s^2+\dots$
The truncation requires }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\cite{ruffini2025ham} gives
$\Sig(v^*+s)\approx\Sig(v^*)+\Sig'(v^*)s+\half\Sig''(v^*)s^2+\dots$.
Quadratic mixing leads when }\DIFaddend $|s|\ll1/\rho\approx1.8$~mV and
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{a nonzero second derivative
for quadratic mixing to be leading. Larger }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\Sig''(v^*)\ne0$; larger }\DIFaddend excursions require the full \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{transfer }\DIFdelend curve.
The linear term preserves the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{two input frequencies. The quadratic cross term
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{carriers, while
$2\cos\omega_1t\cos\omega_2t=\cos\Omega t+\cos(\omega_1+\omega_2)t$
}\DIFaddend generates their difference\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{through the identity
$2\cos\omega_1t\cos\omega_2t=\cos\Omega t+\cos(\omega_1+\omega_2)t$. Low-pass synaptic filtering suppresses the fast terms after mixing; membrane
filtering, when retained, already shapes the carrier polarization. We isolate
the slow source by a carrier average, before applying its frequency-dependent
network gain(\S\ref{sec:carrier}). The fast
$\cos^2\omega_jt$ terms
average to $\half$ and the sum-frequency term to zero:
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. Averaging over the carrier removes the fast terms
before applying network gain; synaptic filtering suppresses those terms
downstream of detection. Since $\langle\cos^2\omega_jt\rangle_c=\half$,
}\DIFaddend \begin{equation}
\big\langle\Sig(v^*+s)\big\rangle_c
=\Sig(v^*)+\tfrac14\Sig''(v^*)\,(\varepsilon_1^2+\varepsilon_2^2)
+\tfrac12\Sig''(v^*)\,\varepsilon_1\varepsilon_2\cos\Omega t+O(\varepsilon^4),
\label{eq:taylor}
\end{equation}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{so a line appears at exactly the envelope frequency $\Omega$, with }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{giving a steady source from the summed squares and a signed beat-source }\DIFaddend amplitude
\begin{equation}
Y_\Omega\simeq\half\,\Sig''(v^*)\,\varepsilon_1\varepsilon_2 ,
\label{eq:demod}
\end{equation}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Here $Y_\Omega$ is the signed rate amplitude before recurrent feedback, }\DIFdelend in s$^{-1}$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{;
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{before recurrence.
Here }\DIFaddend $\varepsilon$ denotes a common \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{small amplitude scale , }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{small-amplitude scale }\DIFaddend and $\simeq$
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{means to }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{denotes }\DIFaddend leading order in \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the polarizations, here and in what follows.
The
same expansion leaves a constant (DC) term, $\tfrac14\Sig''(\varepsilon_1^2+\varepsilon_2^2)$,
which any read-out of the mean rate must include.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{polarization throughout.
}\DIFaddend

For a dynamic cellular response with synaptic input held fixed, define
the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{effective }\DIFdelend second-order coefficient $\chi^{(2)}$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{by the complex beat amplitude$Y_\Omega$,
}\[
\DIFdel{Y_\Omega\simeq\chi^{(2)}(\omega_2,-\omega_1)\,
\varepsilon_2\varepsilon_1,\qquad
r_\Omega(t)=\operatorname{Re}\!\left[Y_\Omega e^{i\Omega t}\right].
}\]%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{through the complex source amplitude,
}\begin{equation}
\DIFadd{Y_\Omega\simeq\chi^{(2)}(\omega_2,-\omega_1)\,
\varepsilon_2\varepsilon_1,\qquad
r_\Omega(t)=\operatorname{Re}\!\left[Y_\Omega e^{i\Omega t}\right].
\label{eq:chi2}
}\end{equation}\DIFaddend
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Here $i^2=-1$, and $r_\Omega(t)$ is the generated rate component before recurrent feedback.
Thus }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The generated rate $r_\Omega$ precedes recurrence, so }\DIFaddend $\chi^{(2)}$ has units
s$^{-1}$mV$^{-2}$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{when its input is polarization
and its output is physical rate}\DIFdelend . With this cosine-amplitude convention, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{its }\DIFaddend memoryless
limit is \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\chi^{(2)}=\Sig''(v^*)/2$. Cellular processes can contribute to the same coefficient, with model-specific
frequency and statedependence: weak amplitude permits the expansion but does not
remove memory, and }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\Sig''/2$. Cellular history, state, and response times can make
it complex and frequency-dependent; }\DIFaddend symmetry or cancellation can \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{make the quadratic term vanish.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{remove
second-order mixing, leaving higher orders.
}\DIFaddend

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Write $\mathcal A(t)$ for the nonnegative envelope of the local two-tone
polarization, in mV.
The quadratic source follows $\mathcal A^2$, whose temporal
and spatial maps are derived in \S\ref{sec:envelope}.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Dense perturbative sweeps use an amplitude-modulated (AM) surrogate that matches
the leading beat source; its waveform and the two-tone controls are specified in
\S\ref{sec:waveforms}.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The same quadratic intermodulation generates endogenous AM sidebands from slow
and fast inputs \cite{ruffini2025ham}, or $\omega_0\pm\Omega$ sidebands when the
recovered beat mixes with an intrinsic rhythm $\omega_0$
(Fig.~\ref{fig:qif_timing}b).
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Equation~\eqref{eq:demod} carries three predictions, all verified below: the
generated beat is quadratic in the drive ($Y_\Omega\propto\varepsilon^2$,
square-law detection); it follows the sigmoid curvature
($Y_\Omega\propto\Sig''(v^*)$, hence zero }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The static detector predicts quadratic scaling, a vanishing source }\DIFaddend at the
inflection\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$v^*=v_0$); and, \emph{at
fixed post-coupling polarization} $\varepsilon$, it is independent of the carrier $\omega_c$. This last prediction needs care: as a function of the \emph{applied} fieldthe
response inherits the coupling $\varepsilon=\lambda_f E_0$,
with applied amplitude $E_0$,
so }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, and carrier independence at fixed polarization. At fixed field,
access attenuation raises }\DIFaddend the field required for a given \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{effect rises with $f_c$ (\S\ref{sec:khz})}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{source}\DIFaddend , consistent
with \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the carrier
dependence of TI thresholds in \cite{opancar2025,peripheral2023}.
Carrier independence is thus a statement about the recovered beat component at fixed
polarization, not about the applied field.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\subsection{\DIFdel{The exact mean field connects static and dynamic mixing}}%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{sec:dynamic}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The adiabatic condition in \S\ref{sec:nmm} determines whether the static curve describes
carrier mixing. Consider an uncoupled MPR population (Eq.~\eqref{eq:mpr}, membrane
time units), with constant drive $Q$ and a weak added current $I_{\rm ac}\cos(\tilde\omega\tilde t)$.
Here $\tilde\omega=\omega\tau_m$ is the normalized carrier angular frequency.
To second order in $I_{\rm ac}$, the carrier-averaged shift of $\tilde r$ is $I_{\rm ac}^2\kappa_{\rm DC}(Q,\tilde\omega)$, where
}\begin{displaymath}
\DIFdel{\kappa_{\rm DC}(Q,\tilde\omega)=\frac{\tilde r^*\,[\tilde\omega^2+3d^2-\nu^2]}{(d^2+\nu^2)\{[d^2+\nu^2-\tilde\omega^2]^2+4d^2\tilde\omega^2\}},
\qquad \tilde r^*=F(Q),\quad d=\frac{\Delta}{\pi \tilde r^*},\quad \nu=2\pi \tilde r^*.
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{The physical rate shift is $I_{\rm ac}^2\kappa_{\rm DC}/\tau_m$.
The fixed-point eigenvalues in normalized time are $-d\pm i\nu$: $d$ is the relaxation rate and $\nu$
the ringing angular frequency, both in membrane-time units; their physical
values are $d/\tau_m$ and $\nu/\tau_m$. The expansion about the fixed point is given in
App.~\ref{app:mpr-mixing}. Its limits are
}\begin{displaymath}
\DIFdel{\kappa_{\rm DC}(Q,\tilde\omega)\xrightarrow[\ \tilde\omega\to0\ ]{}\tfrac14F''(Q),\qquad
\kappa_{\rm DC}(Q,\tilde\omega)\xrightarrow[\ \tilde\omega\gg d,\nu\ ]{}\frac{F'(Q)}{2\tilde\omega^2}.
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{In the first limit the population tracks its static curve, whose curvature rectifies
the input. In the second, the voltage excursion is approximately $I_{\rm ac}/\tilde\omega$; its
square supplies a slow drive that the population reads through $F'$. Above the
inflection of $F$, these limits have opposite signs and the coefficient vanishes at
$\tilde\omega_z=\sqrt{\nu^2-3d^2}$ (Fig.~\ref{fig:crossover}a). The $300$~Hz and kHz carriers
are on the fast side for the parameters of the pyramidal--interneuron network gamma
(PING) circuit used below. That classification is
parameter-dependent: a shorter effective membrane time can bring the same physical
carrier into the adiabatic regime.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Membrane access is already included in this calculation. The linear voltage response
is $H_v(\tilde\omega)=(i\tilde\omega+d)/[(i\tilde\omega+d)^2+\nu^2]$. Dividing by its squared magnitude
gives the mixing coefficient at fixed voltage excursion,
}\begin{displaymath}
\DIFdel{\kappa_V(Q,\tilde\omega)=\frac{\kappa_{\rm DC}(Q,\tilde\omega)}{|H_v(\tilde\omega)|^2}
=\frac{F'(Q)}{2}\;\frac{\tilde\omega^2+3d^2-\nu^2}{\tilde\omega^2+d^2}.
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{This coefficient tends to $F'/2$ at high frequency. The $1/\tilde\omega^2$ suppression
at fixed current comes from $|H_v|^2$, the membrane filtering already represented
by Eq.~\eqref{eq:doors}; it is not an additional detector filter. A static curve
placed after the same membrane has the same leading carrier roll-off, although
its mixing coefficient and sign can differ. The steady-state curvature is
$F''=4\tilde r^*(3d^2-\nu^2)/(d^2+\nu^2)^3$, so the inflection occurs at $\nu^2=3d^2$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{carrier-dependent thresholds \cite{opancar2025,peripheral2023}}\DIFaddend .

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{For nearby fast tones, the quadratic terms in Eq.~\eqref{eq:mpr} contribute at
different orders: $v^2$ at $\omega^{-2}$, $\tilde r v$ at $\omega^{-3}$, and $\tilde r^2$ at
$\omega^{-4}$. Table~\ref{tab:exponents} compares the carrier and beat routes to
the firing rate for a static curve and a dynamic population with the same membrane access.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < \begin{table}[h]
%DIFDELCMD < \centering\small
%DIFDELCMD < \begin{tabular}{lcc}
%DIFDELCMD < \toprule
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelFL{stage }%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{static curve, same membrane }%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{dynamic population }%DIFDELCMD < \\
%DIFDELCMD < \midrule
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelFL{point-membrane current $\to$ voltage }%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{$\omega^{-1}$ }%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{$\omega^{-1}$ }%DIFDELCMD < \\
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelFL{voltage $\to$ rate, carrier }%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{$1$ }%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{$\omega^{-1}$ }%DIFDELCMD < \\
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelFL{current $\to$ rate, carrier }%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{$\omega^{-1}$ }%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{$\omega^{-2}$ }%DIFDELCMD < \\
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelFL{two tones $\to$ beat in the rate }%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{$(\omega_1\omega_2)^{-1}$ }%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{$(\omega_1\omega_2)^{-1}\times$ const. }%DIFDELCMD < \\
%DIFDELCMD < \bottomrule
%DIFDELCMD < \end{tabular}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%
{%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelFL{Fast-carrier scaling with matched point-membrane access, at fixed beat frequency
and a nonzero leading mixing coefficient. The dynamic population integrates the
carrier once more between voltage and rate; the beat is formed before that step.
Prefactors depend on the operating point and beat-frequency susceptibility.}}
%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{tab:exponents}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{table}
%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The first row gives the shared membrane factor $1/\omega$. The second converts
voltage to rate: a static curve adds its slope, whereas the dynamic rate equation
integrates the carrier once more. Combining these stages gives the third-row
carrier responses, $\omega^{-1}$ and $\omega^{-2}$, respectively.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The beat in the last row is formed before that additional rate integration:
static curvature and dynamic $v^2$ each mix the attenuated voltage, retaining
$(\omega_1\omega_2)^{-1}$. Equal exponents do not imply equal amplitudes; mixing
and the susceptibility at fixed $\Omega$ determine the prefactors. For the
uncoupled carrier-averaged response, $\kappa_V$ in Eq.~\eqref{eq:kfixed} tends to
$F'/2$, but is not the full finite-$\Omega$ network prefactor. Thus the dynamic
rate attenuates the carrier twice while forming the beat between those stages.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\subsection{\DIFdel{Detector laws and harmonic signatures}}%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{sec:transduction}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{A neural response proportional to the squared envelope is not equivalent to one
proportional to the envelope. A detector implements some transduction $T(\mathcal A)$ of the local
envelope $\mathcal A(t)$, and the two laws predict different amplitude scaling, harmonic content and
spatial weighting.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{For a narrowband field $s(t)=\mathcal A(t)\cos\omega_c t$ passing through a memoryless nonlinearity
$g$, the low-frequency (baseband) output $Y_{\rm LF}$ is the average of $g$ over one
carrier cycle, with carrier phase $\theta$:
}\[
\DIFdel{Y_{\rm LF}(\mathcal A)=(2\pi)^{-1}\!\int_0^{2\pi}\!g(v^*+\mathcal A\cos\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\theta.
}\]%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{Odd powers of $\mathcal A$ average away, leaving only even ones:
}\begin{displaymath}
\DIFdel{Y_{\rm LF}(\mathcal A)=g(v^*)+\tfrac14 g''(v^*)\mathcal A^2+\tfrac1{64}g^{(4)}(v^*)\mathcal A^4+\cdots
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{A smooth memoryless response with $g''(v^*)\neq0$ therefore transduces $\mathcal A^2$ at
leading weak-input order, whatever its detailed shape. For the JR column, $g=\Sig$, giving the
mechanism of \S\ref{sec:coupling}. Recovering $\mathcal A$ itself requires a qualitatively
different detector: strong rectification. A full-wave rectifier has low-pass-filtered (LPF) output
$\mathrm{LPF}[\,|\mathcal A\cos\omega_c t|\,]=(2/\pi)\mathcal A$ and a half-wave rectifier
$(1/\pi)\mathcal A$, both linear in the envelope, as does a diode-and-capacitor peak detector.
A hard threshold is not one of them: a step detector with offset $\delta>0$ transduces
$\pi^{-1}\arccos(\delta/\mathcal A)$ for $\mathcal A>\delta$ and nothing below it, which is neither linear
nor quadratic in $\mathcal A$ (App.~\ref{app:bound}). Asymmetric channel rectification, threshold
crossing and release events are candidate biological realizations of rectifiers of one
kind or another.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Envelope following and square-law detection are distinguishable at a single site. At a matched target,
$\mathcal A(t)=2\varepsilon|\cos(\Omega t/2)|$, whose Fourier series
$|\cos\theta|=\tfrac2\pi+\tfrac4\pi\sum_{n\ge1}\tfrac{(-1)^{n+1}}{4n^2-1}\cos(2n\theta)$ gives a
harmonic ladder whose nonnegative Fourier amplitudes $A_{n\Omega}$ obey $A_{2\Omega}/A_\Omega=1/5$, $A_{3\Omega}/A_\Omega=3/35$ and
$A_{4\Omega}/A_\Omega=1/21$. The square-law output $\half \mathcal A^2=\varepsilon^2(1+\cos\Omega
t)$ contains only DC and $\Omega$. For the mixed family $T(\mathcal A)=c_1\mathcal A+c_2\mathcal A^2$, with
$c_1\ne0$ and a nonzero fundamental, the
pre-network ratio is
}\begin{displaymath}
\DIFdel{\mathcal H_{\rm det}\equiv\left|\frac{Y_{2\Omega}}{Y_\Omega}\right|
=\frac{1/5}{\left|1+\tfrac{3\pi}{4}\,c_2\varepsilon/c_1\right|},
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{so measuring $\mathcal H_{\rm det}$ constrains $c_2\varepsilon/c_1$ within this family.}\footnote{\DIFdel{The inverse has two branches unless the sign of the fundamental
relative to the linear term is known; for $c_2/c_1\ge0$ it is unique.}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{footnote}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{A smooth detector generates a second harmonic beyond quadratic order,
$Y_{2\Omega}/Y_\Omega\sim(c_4/c_2)\varepsilon^2$, where
$c_2=g''(v^*)/4$ and $c_4=g^{(4)}(v^*)/64$ are the coefficients in
Eq.~\eqref{eq:cycavg}. The square-law signature is a
\emph{vanishing} ratio in the weak-field limit, not an exact zero. The recurrent
network also multiplies each harmonic by its frequency-dependent gain $G$,
$\mathcal H_{\rm meas}=\mathcal H_{\rm det}\,|G(2\Omega)/G(\Omega)|$.
Near resonance, harmonic suppression can make an envelope follower appear square-law.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{An amplitude sweep tests the scaling rather than one ratio. An
envelope-following detector gives $Y_\Omega\propto\varepsilon$ with the harmonic ratio
pinned at $1/5$; a weak square-law detector gives $Y_\Omega\propto\varepsilon^2$ with a
ratio growing as $\varepsilon^2$. A threshold detector is a third class: its
response vanishes for $2\varepsilon\le\delta$, and its harmonic ratio varies
with amplitude after onset. A single harmonic ratio cannot distinguish all
three; amplitude dependence is the primary test.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \subsection{Envelope oscillations and spatial weighting}\label{sec:envelope}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The same two-tone field gives different spatial dose maps under envelope }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Envelope }\DIFaddend following and quadratic detection \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. The distinction is the detector's response to the oscillating envelope, not a change in the applied field. Write }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{assign different spatial doses
to the same field. At position }\DIFaddend $\mathbf x$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{for position and }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, let }\DIFaddend $\varepsilon_j(\mathbf x)$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{for the signed local
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{be
the signed }\DIFaddend polarization of tone $j$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, projected }\DIFdelend along a fixed direction. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{These amplitudes vary
in space and are held fixed only during the temporal averages at one location.
}\DIFdelend The nonnegative
envelope \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{of the two-tone polarization }\DIFaddend is
\[
\mathcal A(\mathbf x,t)=\sqrt{\varepsilon_1(\mathbf x)^2+\varepsilon_2(\mathbf x)^2
+2\varepsilon_1(\mathbf x)\varepsilon_2(\mathbf x)\cos\Omega t}.
\]
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Carrier averaging of the squared signal }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{At each location, low-pass filtering (LPF) removes the carrier terms and }\DIFaddend gives
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{LPF}[s^2(t)]=\tfrac12\mathcal A^2(t)
=\frac{\varepsilon_1^2+\varepsilon_2^2}{2}+\varepsilon_1\varepsilon_2\cos\Omega t,
\label{eq:sqenv}
\end{equation}
with spatial arguments suppressed\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{at a fixed location. Thus square-law detection recovers $\mathcal A^2$,
which }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. The squared envelope }\DIFaddend is sinusoidal at
$\Omega$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, whereas }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{; }\DIFaddend the envelope itself is not\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{: at a matched site, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. For matched tones it is the rectified
cosine }\DIFaddend $\mathcal A(t)=2\varepsilon|\cos(\Omega t/2)|$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, a rectified
cosine }\DIFdelend .
The conventional TI map \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{describes }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{measures }\DIFaddend its excursion,
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathcal{M}[y]\equiv\max_t y-\min_t y$, along the chosen direction \cite{grossman2017}.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\mathcal M[y]\equiv\max_t y-\min_t y$ \cite{grossman2017}.
}

\DIFaddend Table~\ref{tab:transduction-maps} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{compares this envelope with the squared-envelope
signal; the overbar denotes a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{separates these geometric quantities from
detector gains; overbars denote }\DIFaddend beat-period \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{mean. The geometric maps precede detector gains: an }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{means. An }\DIFaddend ideal envelope follower
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{multiplies $\mathcal A$ by its specified }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{outputs $c_1\mathcal A$ for }\DIFaddend gain $c_1$, whereas \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the weak quadratic response change is }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{a smooth transfer $g$ with
nonzero curvature gives }\DIFaddend $g''(v^*)\mathcal A^2/4$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(Eq.~\eqref{eq:cycavg}); for the JR column, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{at weak input (}\DIFaddend $g=\Sig$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{.
The last row carries these gains into the signed steady source. In particular,
the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{in JR).
Thus the }\DIFaddend nonnegative mean-square \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{polarization }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{map }\DIFaddend $\mathcal I$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{alone does not specify
excitation
or suppression.
Envelope following is an additional response assumption; the conventional
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{specifies neither excitation
nor suppression, and an }\DIFaddend envelope-excursion map alone \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{does not specify a neuron's }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{specifies no }\DIFaddend mean output.
\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Envelope following is itself a response assumption.
}\DIFaddend

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering\small
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.22\textwidth}
 >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.34\textwidth}
 >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.36\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
 & Envelope following & Quadratic detection \\
\midrule
Slow signal & $\mathcal A(\mathbf x,t)$ & $\tfrac12\mathcal A(\mathbf x,t)^2$ \\
Mean before gain & $\overline{\mathcal A}(\mathbf x)$
 & $\mathcal I(\mathbf x)=\dfrac{\varepsilon_1^2+\varepsilon_2^2}{2}$ \\
Modulation excursion & $2\min(|\varepsilon_1|,|\varepsilon_2|)$
 & $2|\varepsilon_1\varepsilon_2|$ \\
Units before detector gain & mV & mV$^2$ \\
Signed steady source (output units) & $c_1\overline{\mathcal A}$
 & $Y_{\rm DC}=\tfrac12g''(v^*)\mathcal I$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Envelope and quadratic maps at the same position $\mathbf x$.
The first four rows give geometric quantities before detector gain; the last
gives the steady source after gain, before recurrent feedback. Its sign depends
on the detector. Averaging the slow signal over a beat period gives its mean.
Excursion means maximum minus minimum, not Fourier amplitude.}
\label{tab:transduction-maps}
\end{table}

The excursion maps in Table~\ref{tab:transduction-maps} are related pointwise by
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{M}\big[\tfrac12\mathcal A^2\big]=\max(|\varepsilon_1|,|\varepsilon_2|)\;\mathcal{M}[\mathcal A].
\label{eq:reweight}
\end{equation}
The \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{square-law map is the conventional envelope-excursion map multiplied, at each point,
by the dominant local polarization magnitude. It need not preserve the spatial contrast
of the conventional TI }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{dominant polarization reweights the envelope }\DIFaddend map. Both maps can \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{nevertheless }\DIFdelend have a
central \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{modulation
maximum, as the illustrative line profile }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{maximum, although the quadratic example }\DIFaddend in Fig.~\ref{fig:spatial_maps}a
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{shows;
the quadratic profile is broaderin that example. Where the dominant carrier is larger superficially than at depth,
this reweighting degrades deep-to-superficial contrast. As an illustrative example, at
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{is broader. A larger superficial dominant carrier degrades deep contrast:
for }\DIFaddend $(\varepsilon_1,\varepsilon_2)=(10,1)$ superficially \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{against }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and }\DIFaddend $(0.5,0.5)$
at \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{a matched
deep target, $\mathcal{M}[\mathcal A]$ favors the superficialpoint by $2\times$ and $\mathcal{M}[\half \mathcal A^2]$ by $40\times$. Focality must therefore be computed from the product map, whose spatial ranking
can differ from the envelope map's. Its contrast depends on the actual vector
field distribution: a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{depth, the superficial/deep excursion ratios are $2$ for the envelope
and $40$ for the quadratic map. A }\DIFaddend sufficiently weak second carrier can
\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{instead }\DIFaddend suppress the superficial product. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The finite-element comparison in \S\ref{sec:fem-results}
quantifies this reweighting on one realistic TI montage: at balanced currents,
the target-to-reference contrast is 1.22 for the
envelope and 1.08 for the
square root of the quadratic map.
This is a milder change than the illustrative
two-site contrast above; the line profile is not a head-model calculation.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Actual contrast requires the
vector fields.
}\DIFaddend

With \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{one fixed electrode pair per carrier and fixed neural state, changing the
current ratio rescales the leading quadratic beat map without moving its maxima.
For fixed frequencies, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{fixed electrode pairs, frequencies, neural state, }\DIFaddend linear access, and
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{fixed }\DIFdelend projection directions, write
$\varepsilon_j(\mathbf x)=I_j^{\rm pair}\mathcal P_j(\mathbf x)$,
where $I_j^{\rm pair}>0$ is \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the }\DIFdelend peak pair current (mA) and $\mathcal P_j$ is
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the signed local polarization per unit }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{signed polarization per }\DIFaddend current (mV/mA). Then
\[
\mathcal M[\tfrac12\mathcal A^2]
=2I_1^{\rm pair}I_2^{\rm pair}|\mathcal P_1\mathcal P_2|.
\]
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The normalized map and its ratios between fixed target and reference regions are unchanged, as
Cassar\`a et al.\ noted for second-order mixing }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Changing the ratio therefore rescales the beat map without moving its maxima
or changing normalized maps and target/reference ratios
}\DIFaddend \cite{cassara2025part2}. Fixed detector coefficients and linear \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{network propagation
preserve this }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{propagation
preserve the }\DIFaddend common current factor, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{including for }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{even with }\DIFaddend dynamic mixing and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{spatially }\DIFdelend heterogeneous
gains. Steering \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{within this regime requires changing at least one carrier 's }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{requires a changed carrier }\DIFaddend field shape, for example \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{by redistributing current among several electrodes at that frequency.
The mean-square polarization map, however, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{through
current redistribution among electrodes at one frequency.
}

\DIFadd{The steady source provides a way for current-ratio changes to reshape the
beat response: it }\DIFaddend weights $\mathcal P_1^2$ and $\mathcal P_2^2$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{separately }\DIFdelend by the squared
currents \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. It changes with the ratio;
after signed detection, the resulting steady source can indirectly reshape the beat response by shifting local }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and can thereby shift the }\DIFaddend operating points. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Threshold effects can also break the quadratic prediction; the conventional }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Such state changes fall
outside the fixed-state argument above. Threshold effects also depart from
the weak quadratic regime; the }\DIFaddend $2\min$ steering law \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{applies
when the detector follows the envelope . An }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{requires envelope following.
}

\DIFadd{The product law permits an }\DIFaddend interior beat maximum\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{remains
compatible with the fixed-state product law. }%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The network supplies dynamical selectivity in addition to this spatial weighting }\DIFdelend . \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Writing
$R_\Omega(\mathbf{x})$ for the complex demodulated response at the envelope frequency $\Omega$
produced at location $\mathbf{x}$, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Its spatial weighting also
depends on local detector and network factors (Eq.~\eqref{eq:receiver}), so
resonance can compensate for an unfavorable product only where the source
is nonzero.
}

\DIFadd{The scalar maps use a common fixed projection,
$\varepsilon_j=\boldsymbol\lambda\cdot\mathbf E_j$; the beat changes phase
when either projection changes sign. The current-product scaling also holds
when a detector combines input from several compartments. Let $a_j^k$ be the
complex polarization of carrier $j$ in compartment $k$. A weak dynamic
detector has a bilinear beat source
$Y_\Omega=\sum_{kl}B_{kl}(\omega_2,-\omega_1)a_2^k\overline{a_1^l}$,
where $B_{kl}$ are fixed response coefficients and the overbar means complex
conjugation. A single scalar input is a rank-one specialization. Linear
access gives $a_j^k=I_j^{\rm pair}b_j^k$, with $b_j^k$ the polarization per
unit current, so the common factor $I_1^{\rm pair}I_2^{\rm pair}$ and its
fixed spatial pattern survive without a universal projection vector.
}

\subsection{\DIFadd{Detector laws and harmonic signatures}}\label{sec:transduction}
\DIFadd{For a narrowband input $s(t)=\mathcal A(t)\cos\omega_c t$, a memoryless
nonlinearity $g$ has low-frequency output given by its carrier-cycle average,
}\[
\DIFadd{Y_{\rm LF}(\mathcal A)=(2\pi)^{-1}\!\int_0^{2\pi}\!g(v^*+\mathcal A\cos\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\theta,
}\]
\DIFadd{where $\theta$ is carrier phase. This transduction contains
only even powers:
}\begin{equation}
\DIFadd{Y_{\rm LF}(\mathcal A)=g(v^*)+\tfrac14 g''(v^*)\mathcal A^2+\tfrac1{64}g^{(4)}(v^*)\mathcal A^4+\cdots
\label{eq:cycavg}
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{Thus smooth detection with nonzero curvature is quadratic at weak input.
A threshold detector instead gives
$\pi^{-1}\arccos(\delta/\mathcal A)$ for envelope amplitude
$\mathcal A>\delta$ and zero below, where $\delta>0$ is its offset.
Other rectifier laws are compared in the supplement (\S\ref{supp:harmonics}).
}

\DIFadd{At matched tones, envelope following is linear in amplitude with
second/fundamental harmonic ratio $1/5$. Quadratic detection gives only
the steady term and fundamental; its quartic correction gives
$Y_{2\Omega}/Y_\Omega\sim(c_4/c_2)\varepsilon^2$, with
$c_2=g''/4$ }\DIFaddend and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$G_{\mathbf{x}}(\Omega)$ for the local network
susceptibility there, }\begin{displaymath}
\DIFdel{R_\Omega(\mathbf{x})\;\propto\;\Sig''(v^*_{\mathbf{x}})\;G_{\mathbf{x}}(\Omega)\;
\varepsilon_1(\mathbf{x})\,\varepsilon_2(\mathbf{x}),
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{a product of three factors.
Field overlap sets where a difference-frequency drive is generated at all; the operating point and the network susceptibility set which of the
driven populations amplifies it.
Resonance does not override an unfavorable field
product, but it can compensate for one.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$c_4=g^{(4)}/64$. A threshold detector is silent for
$2\varepsilon\le\delta$ and has an amplitude-dependent ratio above onset.
The full harmonic series and mixed-detector inversion are supplementary
(\S\ref{supp:harmonics}).
}\ifjne\else
\subsection{\DIFadd{Harmonic ladder and mixed detectors}}\label{supp:harmonics}
\DIFadd{Full- and half-wave rectifiers yield $2\mathcal A/\pi$ and
$\mathcal A/\pi$, respectively; a diode-and-capacitor detector follows the
peak. Channel rectification, threshold crossing, and release events are
possible biological implementations.
}\DIFaddend

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Equation~\eqref{eq:spatial} is written for scalar projected fields. In general the coupling is vectorial and compartment-specific, $\varepsilon_i(\mathbf{x})=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\!\cdot\!\mathbf{E}_i(\mathbf{x})$, so the
mixing product $(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\!\cdot\!\mathbf{E}_1)
(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\!\cdot\!\mathbf{E}_2)$ is signed: its magnitude follows the
product
of the projected amplitudes , while its phase inverts with
either the
sign of $\Sig''$ or
the relative sign of the two projections.The derivations below take the collinear,
scalar case. }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Envelope following and square-law detection are distinguishable at a single site. At a matched target,
$\mathcal A(t)=2\varepsilon|\cos(\Omega t/2)|$, whose Fourier series
$|\cos\theta|=\tfrac2\pi+\tfrac4\pi\sum_{n\ge1}\tfrac{(-1)^{n+1}}{4n^2-1}\cos(2n\theta)$ gives a
harmonic ladder whose nonnegative Fourier amplitudes $A_{n\Omega}$ obey $A_{2\Omega}/A_\Omega=1/5$, $A_{3\Omega}/A_\Omega=3/35$ and
$A_{4\Omega}/A_\Omega=1/21$. The square-law output $\half \mathcal A^2=\varepsilon^2(1+\cos\Omega
t)$ contains only DC and $\Omega$. For the mixed family $T(\mathcal A)=c_1\mathcal A+c_2\mathcal A^2$, with
$c_1\ne0$ and a nonzero fundamental, the
pre-network ratio is
}\begin{equation}
\DIFadd{\mathcal H_{\rm det}\equiv\left|\frac{Y_{2\Omega}}{Y_\Omega}\right|
=\frac{1/5}{\left|1+\tfrac{3\pi}{4}\,c_2\varepsilon/c_1\right|},
\label{eq:rdet}
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{so measuring $\mathcal H_{\rm det}$ constrains $c_2\varepsilon/c_1$ within this family.}\footnote{\DIFadd{The inverse has two branches unless the sign of the fundamental
relative to the linear term is known; for $c_2/c_1\ge0$ it is unique.}}
\DIFaddend

\DIFaddbegin \fi

\DIFadd{The network modifies these ratios through
$\mathcal H_{\rm meas}=\mathcal H_{\rm det}|G(2\Omega)/G(\Omega)|$.
Near resonance it can suppress an envelope follower's second harmonic,
making it appear quadratic. Amplitude sweeps and gain correction are
therefore needed; a threshold detector can match one harmonic ratio, so that
ratio alone cannot identify the detector.
}

\DIFaddend \subsection{The steady source has a state-dependent sign}\label{sec:dc}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Mean-square polarization is a nonnegative geometric map. Detection converts it
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Detection converts nonnegative mean-square polarization }\DIFaddend into a signed steady
source\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, and the circuit }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{; recurrence }\DIFaddend converts that source into a mean response. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Under uniform conductivity, access, and detection, the source's
largest magnitude lies on compartment boundaries; the envelope-following mean
obeys the same restriction, as shown below.
The envelope mean is
}\[
\DIFdel{\overline{\mathcal A}(\mathbf x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}
\sqrt{\varepsilon_1(\mathbf x)^2+\varepsilon_2(\mathbf x)^2
+2\varepsilon_1(\mathbf x)\varepsilon_2(\mathbf x)\cos\vartheta}\,\mathrm d\vartheta,
}\]%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{where $\vartheta$ is beat phase. At a matched site with
$\varepsilon_1=\varepsilon_2=\varepsilon>0$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{``DC-like''
describes this source, not the applied field, which has no DC component.
At matched polarizations $\varepsilon>0$}\DIFaddend ,
the envelope and quadratic means are $4\varepsilon/\pi$ and
$\varepsilon^2$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, respectively. The applied field has no DC
component: these means describe the ideal detector outputs after multiplication
by their gains , and the quadratic response change takes the sign of $g''$. Even one carrier gives either ideal detector }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{before detector gains (supplement, \S\ref{supp:envelope-proof}). Either ideal detector gives }\DIFaddend a mean
output \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{with one carrier}\DIFaddend , but no beat\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{modulation}\DIFdelend ; adaptation can alter the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{physiological }\DIFdelend mean response.

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{For the JR quadratic detector, the local rate source in Eq.~\eqref{eq:taylor} is
}\[
\DIFdel{Y_{\rm DC}(\mathbf x,\mathbf n)
=\frac{\Sig''(v^*_{\mathbf x})}{2}\,\mathcal I(\mathbf x,\mathbf n),
\qquad
\mathcal I(\mathbf x,\mathbf n)
=\frac{\varepsilon_1(\mathbf x,\mathbf n)^2+
\varepsilon_2(\mathbf x,\mathbf n)^2}{2}\ge0.
}\]%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The static JR source is
}\[
\DIFadd{Y_{\rm DC}(\mathbf x,\mathbf n)
=\frac{\Sig''(v^*_{\mathbf x})}{2}\,\mathcal I(\mathbf x,\mathbf n),
\qquad
\mathcal I(\mathbf x,\mathbf n)
=\frac{\varepsilon_1(\mathbf x,\mathbf n)^2+
\varepsilon_2(\mathbf x,\mathbf n)^2}{2}\ge0,
}\]\DIFaddend
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Here }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{where }\DIFaddend $v^*_{\mathbf x}$ is the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{baseline voltage at position $\mathbf x$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{local baseline }\DIFaddend and $\mathbf n$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{is the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{the common
}\DIFaddend unit cell orientation\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{used for both signed polarizations. The source $Y_{\rm DC}$ is positive on the convex part of the rate curve,
negative on its concave part, and zero to this order }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. Convex and concave rate curves give positive and
negative sources, respectively; the quadratic source vanishes }\DIFaddend at the inflection
\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and in the saturated tails}\DIFaddend .
Reversing either carrier \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{'s polarity }\DIFdelend or $\mathbf n$ leaves the squares unchanged;
other \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{directions change their magnitudes , while the sign remains set by the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{orientations change magnitudes but not the sign at }\DIFaddend fixed curvature.
Figure~\ref{fig:spatial_maps}c shows both signs \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{using }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{on }\DIFaddend one positive normalization.
At the pinned JR \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{operating point }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{state, }\DIFaddend $v^*=8.30$~mV, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Sig''<0$, so the static
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{the }\DIFaddend source lowers the local rate
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{.
The observed mean-rate change also depends on which populations receive the source and on recurrent feedback; the JR examples in \S\ref{sec:timing} have
a negative mean-rate shift.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The coefficient depends on the detector regime.
For an uncoupled QIF population
at fast carriers, $v^2$ generates a positive effective input shift, and the
readout through $F'>0$ gives a positive rate shift. In the slow-carrier
(adiabatic) limit, $\kappa_{\rm DC}\to F''/4$, so the rate shift is negative
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{because $\Sig''(v^*)<0$.
This curvature rule applies to static detection; dynamic rectification and
recurrence can change the sign of the mean response.
The same operating-point dependence enters the multitone sigmoid expansion for
endogenous infraslow ($<0.1$~Hz) mixing in coupled Jansen--Rit models \cite{Just2026mixing}.
}

\label{sec:opp}
\DIFadd{The Population Excitation--Inhibition Index (PEIX) \cite{ruffini2025cfc}
measures signed departure from maximal sigmoid slope, positive }\DIFaddend above the
inflection \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{of $F$ (Eq.~\eqref{eq:crossover_limits}). The positive
E-drive shift in \S\ref{sec:qif} is therefore a fast-carrier result for that
model; the mean-rate response after recurrent
feedback need not retain its sign}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and negative below; its definition and curvature identities are
in Appendix~\ref{app:peix}. Changes in $v^*$ through neuromodulation,
attention, arousal, psychedelic exposure, or dysfunction of PV interneurons
in Alzheimer's disease can scale or reverse the static JR source.
Dynamic detectors have their own state-dependent coefficients and susceptibilities, without
a universal PEIX sign rule; state sweeps isolate detection only if recurrent
gain is also measured}\DIFaddend .

Carrier averaging retains the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{cross term as the beat; averaging over many beat cycles }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{beat cross term; a beat-period average }\DIFaddend removes
it, leaving \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{a sum of }\DIFdelend intensities without cross-frequency cancellation. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{At the detector output, where }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Where
}\DIFaddend $Y_{\rm DC}\ne0$,
$|Y_\Omega/Y_{\rm DC}|=2|\varepsilon_1\varepsilon_2|/
(\varepsilon_1^2+\varepsilon_2^2)\le1$, with equality at matched \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{tone }\DIFdelend magnitudes.
A common access factor cancels\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{from this local ratio}\DIFdelend . In the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{polarization
mean-square map $\mathcal I=(\varepsilon_1^2+\varepsilon_2^2)/2$, the example above gives }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{superficial/deep example of \S\ref{sec:envelope}, $\mathcal I$ is
}\DIFaddend $50.5$ superficially and $0.25$ at depth, a $202$-fold contrast;
the \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{corresponding }\DIFaddend beat/DC ratios are $0.20$ and $1$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, respectively}\DIFdelend .

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The geometric restriction follows in }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{In }\DIFaddend a homogeneous, isotropic, source-free conductor\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. Writing $\phi_j$ for the potential of tone $j$}\DIFdelend , the potentials $\phi_j$,
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{each component of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{field components }\DIFaddend $\mathbf E_j=-\nabla\phi_j$, and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the
signed }\DIFdelend polarizations
$\varepsilon_j=\boldsymbol\lambda\cdot\mathbf E_j$ for spatially fixed
coupling $\boldsymbol\lambda$ are harmonic. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Thus
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Consequently
}\DIFaddend $\nabla^2\mathcal I=\sum_j|\nabla\varepsilon_j|^2\ge0$:
the intensity \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{map is subharmonicand has }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{is subharmonic, with }\DIFaddend no strict interior maximum. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{A montage can shape each
carrier field, but the maximum
of this map }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Its maximum
}\DIFaddend lies on the region's boundary\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{;
conductivity interfacesare such boundaries. For a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, including conductivity interfaces, regardless
of montage. With }\DIFaddend spatially constant nonzero \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{detector coefficient, this bounds the magnitude
of $Y_{\rm DC}$. A negative coefficient makes the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{detection, the same holds for
$|Y_{\rm DC}|$; negative curvature makes the boundary }\DIFaddend source most negative\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{at the
boundary}\DIFdelend .
Spatially varying \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{access, orientation, or detector }\DIFaddend coefficients require a
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{separately weighted map}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{weighted map, and the restriction does not extend to the physiological mean
response or an indefinite multicompartment DC form}\DIFaddend .

The envelope mean \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{has the same restriction}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{is also subharmonic: at each beat phase it is a norm of
harmonic components, and phase averaging preserves that property (supplement,
\S\ref{supp:envelope-proof}).
}\ifjne\else
\subsection{\DIFadd{Envelope-mean subharmonicity}}\label{supp:envelope-proof}
\DIFadd{The envelope mean is
}\[
\DIFadd{\overline{\mathcal A}(\mathbf x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}
\sqrt{\varepsilon_1(\mathbf x)^2+\varepsilon_2(\mathbf x)^2
+2\varepsilon_1(\mathbf x)\varepsilon_2(\mathbf x)\cos\vartheta}\,\mathrm d\vartheta,
}\]
\DIFadd{where $\vartheta$ is beat phase.
}

\DIFadd{For fixed scalar coupling in homogeneous, isotropic, source-free tissue,
the envelope mean is subharmonic}\DIFaddend . At each beat phase $\vartheta$,
$\mathcal A$ is the Euclidean norm of
$(\varepsilon_1+\varepsilon_2\cos\vartheta,\,\varepsilon_2\sin\vartheta)$,
a vector of harmonic functions. A norm is convex, so this envelope is subharmonic;
averaging over $\vartheta$ preserves that property. Thus both idealized mean maps
lack a strict interior maximum under these conditions (Fig.~\ref{fig:spatial_maps}b).
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{This does not prohibit a beat maximum: the product instead obeys
$\nabla^2(\varepsilon_1\varepsilon_2)=2\nabla\varepsilon_1\cdot\nabla\varepsilon_2$,
whose sign is unrestricted . Within the homogeneous }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin

\fi
\DIFadd{By contrast,
$\nabla^2(\varepsilon_1\varepsilon_2)=2\nabla\varepsilon_1\cdot\nabla\varepsilon_2$
has unrestricted sign. Within this homogeneous scalar }\DIFaddend model, interior
selectivity must \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{therefore }\DIFdelend come from the oscillatory response\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{rather than from }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, not }\DIFaddend either mean map.
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Spatially varying access , orientation, or curvaturecan change the signed-source
map and the resulting physiological mean-response map. The intensity
result does
not extend to these weighted responses.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{With isotropic orientations and orientation-independent access and curvature,
orthogonal fields cancel the averaged beat while retaining steady intensity
(supplement, \S\ref{supp:orientation}).
}\ifjne\else
\subsection{\DIFadd{Isotropic orientation averages}}\label{supp:orientation}
\DIFaddend For isotropically distributed unit orientations
$\mathbf n$ with orientation-independent access and curvature,
$\langle(\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf E_j)^2\rangle=|\mathbf E_j|^2/3$
and $\langle(\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf E_1)(\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf E_2)\rangle
=\mathbf E_1\cdot\mathbf E_2/3$: orthogonal fields cancel the averaged beat,
but retain the DC intensity.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{At equal }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin

\fi
\DIFadd{An unmodulated carrier at the same }\DIFaddend local RMS polarization \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, an unmodulated
carrier }\DIFdelend gives the same
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{mean shift at second order when }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{second-order mean shift if }\DIFaddend its DC mixing coefficient equals those of both
TI carriers. \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{This motivates the equal-RMS carrier control in
\S\ref{sec:qif-methods}. }\DIFaddend For a dynamic detector, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{this requires the frequency-dependent coefficient to vary
negligibly across }\DIFdelend the \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{coefficient must vary
negligibly over the }\DIFaddend carrier interval.

\DIFaddbegin \subsection{\DIFadd{Comparison with direct tDCS and tACS}}\label{sec:direct-comparison}
\DIFadd{We compare TI's steady and beat sources with transcranial direct-current
stimulation (tDCS) and tACS at $\Omega$, respectively, using polarizations
at the same sigmoid entry point. For equal TI tones
$\varepsilon_1=\varepsilon_2=\varepsilon>0$,
}\[
\DIFadd{Y_{\rm DC}^{\rm TI}\simeq Y_\Omega^{\rm TI}
\simeq \frac{1}{2}\Sig''(v^*)\varepsilon^2.
}\]
\DIFadd{A constant polarization $\varepsilon_{\rm D}$ or sinusoid
$\varepsilon_{\rm A}\cos\Omega t$ instead gives
}\[
\DIFadd{Y_{\rm DC}^{\rm tDCS}\simeq\Sig'(v^*)\varepsilon_{\rm D},
\qquad
Y_\Omega^{\rm tACS}\simeq\Sig'(v^*)\varepsilon_{\rm A}.
}\]
\DIFadd{Here $\varepsilon_{\rm D}$ is signed and negative $\varepsilon_{\rm A}$
denotes phase reversal. The equivalent direct polarization matching either
TI source is
}\[
\DIFadd{\varepsilon_{\rm eq}
=\frac{\Sig''(v^*)}{2\Sig'(v^*)}\,\varepsilon^2,
\qquad
\frac{\Sig''(v^*)}{\Sig'(v^*)}
=-\rho\tanh\!\left[\frac{\rho(v^*-v_0)}{2}\right].
}\]
\DIFadd{Sigmoid steepness and operating voltage determine the ratio; the rate ceiling
cancels, and the quadratic TI sources vanish at the inflection.
}

\DIFadd{For the JR parameters $\rho=0.56$~mV$^{-1}$, $v_0=6$~mV,
$e_0=2.5$~s$^{-1}$, and the pinned operating point $v^*=8.30$~mV,
$\Sig'=0.4745$~s$^{-1}$mV$^{-1}$ and
$\Sig''=-0.1508$~s$^{-1}$mV$^{-2}$. With each direct-input amplitude
equal to the per-carrier TI amplitude, the source-magnitude ratio is
$|\varepsilon_{\rm eq}|/\varepsilon
=0.1589\,\varepsilon/(1~\mathrm{mV})$:
$1.59\%$ at $0.1$~mV and $4.77\%$ at $0.3$~mV, rounded to $2$--$5\%$.
At $0.3$~mV per carrier, the equivalent direct polarization is
$-0.0143$~mV, or $-14.3~\mu$V. The negative sign specifies a suppressive
local source for tDCS and an inverted source phase for tACS.
}

\DIFadd{Matching the total TI peak instead uses direct amplitude $2\varepsilon$
and halves these ratios. RMS matching uses tACS amplitude
$\sqrt2\varepsilon$ or tDCS magnitude $\varepsilon$, because TI's RMS
polarization is $\varepsilon$. The tACS quadratic steady source,
$\Sig''\varepsilon_{\rm A}^2/4$, then equals TI's for this static detector.
Equal electrode currents need not match polarization: access differs among
DC, beat and carrier frequencies, and among entry compartments.
}

\DIFadd{At a common state and entry point, matched steady sources share the
zero-frequency transfer, and matched beat and tACS sources share the transfer
at $\Omega$. These transfers differ, and rectification of the amplified TI
beat can add a mean shift.
Source ratios therefore imply neither universal physiological-effect ratios
nor equal spatial responses.
}

\DIFaddend \subsection{Amplification: the synaptic low-pass and the network resonance}\label{sec:carrier}
Synaptic filtering suppresses the carrier after the nonlinearity has generated
the beat. Each JR excitatory synapse has transfer function
$H_s(\omega)=Aa/(i\omega+a)^2$, a second-order low-pass with characteristic
frequency $a/(2\pi)\approx16$~Hz. It passes a $10$~Hz component with relative
amplitude about $0.7$, while reducing a $100$~Hz component to about $2.5\%$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(Fig. ~\ref{fig:carrier}). }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. }\DIFaddend Provided the carrier does not itself excite a responsive
mode, the loop therefore reads predominantly the generated low-frequency drive.

% fig:carrier moved to Results (Theory is equations-only)

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{What turns this recovered beatcomponent into a large, frequency-selective response is
the network. Linearizing the JR column (node) about its }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The recurrent circuit selects the recovered beat. Linearizing about the JR
}\DIFaddend fixed point gives a leading \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{eigenvalue }\DIFdelend pair $\mu_\pm=-\gamma\pm i\omega_0$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{; near the Hopf the response is dominated by this critical
mode, with
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, with
modal }\DIFaddend damping $\gamma$ and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{modal }\DIFdelend frequency $\omega_0$. Below a supercritical
Hopf, $\gamma\to0^+$ and the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{column is a sharply tuned (high quality-factor $\mathcal{Q}=\omega_0/2\gamma$) resonator;
driving it with the demodulated beat component
\eqref{eq:demod} at $\Omega$ gives a resonant response. Writing $G$ for the complex susceptibility, a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{quality factor
$\mathcal Q=\omega_0/(2\gamma)$ grows. A }\DIFaddend constant-numerator two-pole
approximation \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{gives its magnitude as
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{to the complex susceptibility $G$ gives
}\DIFaddend \begin{equation}
|G(\Omega)|\propto\big[(\omega_0^2+\gamma^2-\Omega^2)^2+(2\gamma\Omega)^2\big]^{-1/2},
\qquad |G(\omega_0)|\propto\frac{1}{2\gamma\omega_0}
\quad(\gamma\ll\omega_0),
\label{eq:gain}
\end{equation}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{so the response peaks near $\omega_0$ with }\DIFdelend an approximately Lorentzian power \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{profile and }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{peak near $\omega_0$ with }\DIFaddend $1/\gamma$
growth \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{near }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{at }\DIFaddend a simple Hopf with nonzero input--output projections.\footnote{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Here }\DIFdelend
\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The eigenvalue frequency }\DIFaddend $\omega_0$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{is }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{differs from }\DIFaddend the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{eigenvalue's imaginary part. The
}\DIFdelend canonical oscillator\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{has }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{'s
}\DIFaddend undamped frequency $\sqrt{\omega_0^2+\gamma^2}$ and peak
$\sqrt{\omega_0^2-\gamma^2}$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{for }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{(}\DIFaddend $\omega_0>\gamma$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{; frequency-dependent
network }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{).
Frequency-dependent }\DIFaddend projections can \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{further }\DIFaddend shift the peak.}
This weak-drive law requires \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{stability after }\DIFaddend the rectified mean shift\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{to leave the state stable;
entrainment above the Hopfinstead requires a phase-capture test
(\S\ref{sec:vocab-methods})}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{.
Above the Hopf, entrainment instead means phase capture of an autonomous rhythm}\DIFaddend .

Write $D$ for the detector's beat-source coefficient: for the JR rate source,
$D=\half\Sig''(v^*)$, half the local sigmoid curvature. Combining access,
detection, and amplification gives
\begin{equation}
R_\Omega\simeq G(\Omega;\gamma)\;D\;\lambda_f(\omega_1)E_1\,\lambda_f(\omega_2)E_2 ,
\label{eq:receiver}
\end{equation}
%DIF >  Retain the former spatial-equation label as an alias of the general receiver.
\DIFaddbegin \makeatletter\begingroup\edef\@currentlabel{\theequation}\label{eq:spatial}\endgroup\makeatother
\DIFaddend where $\lambda_f$ maps field to local carrier polarization, $D$ converts the
product of those polarizations into a beat drive, and $G$ maps that drive to the
complex observed response, whose measured amplitude is $A_\Omega=|R_\Omega|$.
For JR, $G$ is the response to a generated rate drive.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{For fast-carrier QIF in the normalized units of Eq.~\eqref{eq:mpr}, $D=1$ for
the $v^2$ source and $G$ includes its conversion through the voltage and rate
dynamics. Thus the population readout is included once. In these limits }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{A static detector has carrier-independent }\DIFaddend $D$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{is
independent of carrier frequency }\DIFdelend at fixed polarization;
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{across the dynamic crossover it acquires }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{a dynamic detector can retain }\DIFaddend frequency dependence. A carrier sweep and a beat sweep
therefore constrain different stages rather than a single effective gain.

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The resonance does not supply the quadratic mixer}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Resonance requires a beat source}\DIFaddend . The cubic Hopf normal form
responds to an in-band drive but has no second-order difference-frequency source\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(App. ~\ref{app:sl}). }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. }\DIFaddend A large resonant susceptibility alone therefore does not
establish beat generation.
\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{In the calibrated noise-free Stuart--Landau networks tested in \cite{Ruffini2026iso},
first-order rate transmission and readout yielded negligible infraslow BOLD power.
}\DIFaddend

\subsection{Carrier access depends on the effective membrane response}\label{sec:khz}
Carrier independence at fixed polarization does not imply independence at fixed
applied field. As an isopotential access model, consider
$H_m(\omega)=1/(1+i\omega\tau_m)$, with effective time constant $\tau_m$.
This is the response of a point membrane to injected current; a field coupled
through a cable need not have the same transfer.
For nearby carriers, the two polarizations acquire nearly the same attenuation.
The quadratic beat then satisfies
\begin{equation}
\frac{|Y_\Omega(f_c)|}{|Y_\Omega^{\rm direct}|}
\simeq |H_m(\omega_c)|^2
=\frac{1}{1+(\omega_c\tau_m)^2}
\sim\frac{1}{(\omega_c\tau_m)^2}
\quad(\omega_c\tau_m\gg1),
\label{eq:khz}
\end{equation}
where $Y_\Omega^{\rm direct}$ is the beat generated with the same static coupling
and no access attenuation. This filter belongs either in the field-to-polarization
map or in the membrane equation that computes that polarization.

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Somatic passive time constants are commonly $10$--$30$~ms; reported human pyramidal
values include approximately $12$~ms \cite{deitcher2017} and $16.5\pm3.7$~ms
\cite{eyal2016}. For the illustrative }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{For an illustrative somatic }\DIFaddend $\tau_m=16$~ms\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{used here, the power factor
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, }\DIFaddend $|H_m|^2$ is about
$10^{-4}$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{at $1$~kHz}\DIFdelend , $2.5\times10^{-5}$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{at $2$~kHz}\DIFdelend , and $10^{-6}$ at \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$1$, $2$, and }\DIFaddend $10$~kHz.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{A static nonlinearity placed after this point-membrane response
therefore receives strongly attenuated carriers.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Cohen et al.'s periaxonal circuit provides a reason to distinguish axonal from
somatic access \cite{cohen2020periaxonal}. A conducting submyelin space, about
$12$~nm wide and partly sealed at the paranodes, separates the voltage across
myelin from that across the axonal membrane. Their minimum fitted axonal decay
was approximately $1$~ms. This is a response to injected current in a distributed
cable, rather than a measured transfer from a TI field. The AIS is another candidate
site, with a high sodium-channel density \cite{kole2008ais}; this property supports
nonlinear sensitivity but does not fix its charging time.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{A shorter effective time changes carrier transmission quantitatively. The sensitivity
case $\tau_m=0.2$~ms gives $|H_m|^2\approx0.39$ at $1$~kHz }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The assumed $0.2$~ms case gives $0.39$ }\DIFaddend and $0.14$ at \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$1$ and }\DIFaddend $2$~kHz
(Fig.~\ref{fig:khz})\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. This assumed time represents the fast-compartment hypothesis
in Eq.~\eqref{eq:doors}, or motivates a shorter membrane time in a QIF reduction; it }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{; this transfer }\DIFaddend has not been measured under TI at \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the AIS or a node. In the latter description, the approach to a static transfer is governed by
Eq.~\eqref{eq:slow_synapse}; the membrane filter must not be applied again outside
the QIF equations. A short access time improves carrier transmission without,
by itself, specifying the mixing coefficient or the remaining circuit resonance.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{an
AIS or node. }\DIFaddend Table~\ref{tab:doors} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{separates measured decays}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{distinguishes measured responses}\DIFaddend ,
assumed access\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{times, strong-pulse charging, cellular detection, and downstream synaptic filtering. A characteristic frequency requires a specified filter; a measured decay alone is not }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, and downstream filtering. Axonal decay alone specifies
neither field coupling nor }\DIFaddend a TI cutoff.

\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{QIF computes access in its membrane equation, requiring no additional filter
on current input. Shorter access improves transmission without determining
mixing or recurrent gain.
}

\centering\small

\midrule

\cite{deitcher2017,eyal2016}. \\

Injected-current responses in Cohen et al.'s periaxonal circuit
\cite{cohen2020periaxonal}; evidence for distinct axonal voltage dynamics. \\

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Cable geometry changes this attenuation: a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{A sufficiently }\DIFaddend long passive cable with a sealed end \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{has a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{in a uniform axial field
has }\DIFaddend quadratic-beat \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{factor of about }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{factors near }\DIFaddend $10^{-2}$ at $1$~kHz and $10^{-3}$ at
$10$~kHz for $\tau_m=16$~ms, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{rather than }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{exceeding }\DIFaddend the point-membrane values\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(App.
~\ref{app:access}, Eq.~\eqref{eq:lambda}).
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{.
}\DIFaddend

already
contains, and where the detector lacks them, as QIF lacks }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Any additional gate filter must represent kinetics absent from the detector
and have a model or measurement behind it. QIF lacks explicit }\DIFaddend sodium-channel
kinetics\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{,
the filter needs its own model or measurement; neither }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{; neither it nor }\DIFaddend a static sigmoid \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{nor QIF
}\DIFdelend specifies a universal $1$~kHz \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{gate cutoff.
Equation~\eqref{eq:crossover} gives the QIF realization of this response.
Carrier sweeps can constrain the
