\DIFdelbegin \subsection{\DIFdel{Operating-point dependence}}%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{sec:opp}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Because $Y_\Omega\propto\Sig''(v^*)$, demodulation is governed by the curvature at
the operating point, and in particular by how far $v^*$ sits from the sigmoid
inflection (Fig. ~\ref{fig:opp}). The Population Excitation--Inhibition Index (PEIX) of \cite{ruffini2025cfc} measures signed departure from maximal sigmoid slope:
}\begin{displaymath}
\DIFdel{\mathrm{PEIX}(v^*)=\operatorname{sign}(v^*-v_0)
\left\{1-\operatorname{sech}^2\!\Big[\tfrac{\rho}{2}\,(v^*-v_0)\Big]\right\}.
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \subsection{\DIFadd{The exact mean field extends the static detector}}\label{sec:dynamic}
\DIFadd{The exact mean field of all-to-all quadratic integrate-and-fire (QIF) neurons
with Lorentzian excitabilities supplies both a static rate curve and its
dynamic extension. For weak carriers at fixed synaptic input, steady
rectification follows the curve's curvature at low frequency and its slope
at high frequency. The Montbri\'o--Paz\'o--Roxin (MPR) equations evolve physical
rate $r$ and dimensionless mean voltage $v$ \cite{montbrio2015}; dynamic
synapses produce a second-generation neural mass (NMM2)
\cite{ruffini2021nmm2,clusella2023}. The population equations are
}\begin{equation}
\DIFadd{\tau_m\dot r=\frac{\Delta}{\pi\tau_m}+2rv,\qquad
\tau_m\dot v=v^2+\bar\eta-(\pi\tau_m r)^2+I(t).
\label{eq:mpr}
}\end{equation}\DIFaddend
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{It is zero at
$v_0$, positive }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Here $\tau_m$ is membrane time, dots denote physical-time derivatives,
$\bar\eta$ and $\Delta>0$ are the excitability center and half-width, and
$I(t)$ is dimensionless input, including recurrent drive when present.
For constant total drive $Q=\bar\eta+I$, equilibrium gives
$v=-\Delta/(2\pi\tau_m r)$ and
}\begin{equation}
\DIFadd{\tau_m r=F(Q)=\frac{1}{\pi}\sqrt{\frac{Q+\sqrt{Q^2+\Delta^2}}{2}} .
\label{eq:Fmpr}
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{Primes on $F$ denote drive derivatives. Below its inflection at
$Q=\Delta/\sqrt3$, curvature reflects recruitment across threshold;
}\DIFaddend above it, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{negative below it, and tends to $\pm1$
in the saturated tails.
The normalized slope is instead
$\Sig'(v^*)/\Sig'(v_0)=1-|\mathrm{PEIX}(v^*)|$, with
$\Sig'(v_0)=e_0\rho/2$. Demodulation follows the curvature,
which vanishes at
the inflection and in the tails and peaks in magnitude on the flanks.
For the
logistic curve, $\Sig''=-\rho\Sig'\operatorname{sign}(\mathrm{PEIX})\sqrt{|\mathrm{PEIX}|}$;
the index specifies the side and saturation, but is not itself the mixing gain }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{the concave square-root rate of active cells. This nonlinearity
exists without recurrence and grows without bound, unlike the JR sigmoid.
}

\DIFadd{If relaxation is faster than synaptic variation, NMM2 reduces to
$r(t)\simeq F(Q(t))/\tau_m$ with dynamically filtered recurrence
\cite{ruffini2021nmm2,clusella2023}. For population relaxation time
$\tau_{\rm rel}$ and synaptic time $\tau_s$ (Eq.~\eqref{eq:slow_synapse},
Appendix~\ref{app:models}), this requires $\tau_{\rm rel}\ll\tau_s$.
Applying the static curve to carriers further requires
$\omega_c\tau_{\rm rel}\ll1$: the same population can track synaptic
input quasistatically while responding dynamically to kHz input.
Recurrent excitatory--inhibitory circuits resonate without intrinsic
cellular resonance \cite{brunelhakim1999,brunelwang2003}. Such synaptic
modes survive the fast-membrane limit, whereas modes requiring finite
membrane dynamics need not.
}

\paragraph{\DIFadd{Field coupling and recurrent gain in the slow limit.}}
\DIFadd{The field input survives this reduction. For one self-coupled population,
write $I=I_b+\tau_mJr_{\rm syn}+\lambda_I E$, where $I_b$ is the normalized
background input, $r_{\rm syn}$ the filtered rate, and $J$ dimensionless
self-coupling. For a fixed field direction, $\lambda_I=\kappa_E/I_s$:
$\kappa_E$ is equivalent current per field and $I_s$ the current unit.
A cellular voltage-polarization coefficient requires an impedance conversion
before entering this current-driven model. At equilibrium $r_{\rm syn}=r$;
differentiating $\tau_mr=F(\bar\eta+I_b+\tau_mJr+\lambda_IE)$ gives
}\begin{equation}
\DIFadd{\frac{\partial r_*}{\partial E}
=\frac{\lambda_I F'(Q_*)}{\tau_m[1-JF'(Q_*)]}.
\label{eq:slow-field-gain}
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{Positive self-feedback amplifies the steady response on the stable branch
$JF'(Q_*)<1$; negative feedback suppresses it. The bare curve $F$ takes
total drive as its argument. A transfer fitted after an internally recurrent
population settles already contains that feedback and must not be coupled
through the same loop again. A JR-like description retains this gain when
input conversion, local slope, and feedback are matched
\cite{ruffini2021nmm2,clusella2023}; its connections between populations do
not automatically specify the same within-population self-coupling}\DIFaddend .

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{This relation is specific to the memoryless logistic detector. In the exact mean field at a fast carrier the rectified DC term
follows the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Outside the adiabatic limit, the quadratic voltage term mixes the carriers
before the rate responds. For an uncoupled population under a weak current
$I_{\rm ac}\cos\omega t$, write the mean-rate shift as
$I_{\rm ac}^2\kappa_{\rm DC}/\tau_m$. At slow carriers,
$\kappa_{\rm DC}\to F''(Q)/4$; at fast carriers,
$\kappa_{\rm DC}\sim F'(Q)/[2(\omega\tau_m)^2]$.
The slow response follows curvature and can be negative above the inflection.
The fast DC coefficient instead follows the positive }\DIFaddend slope $F'$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{and is }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, }\DIFaddend largest at the
inflection\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(\S\ref{sec:dynamic}), so the relation between PEIX and TI efficacy is model-dependent; PEIX remains a measurable
read-out of the operating point.
In the
Jansen--Rit detector, anything shifting $v^*$
(for example, neuromodulation, attention, arousal, psychedelic exposure, or
PV-interneuron dysfunction in
Alzheimer's disease)scales, and can flip the sign of , the TI response; in
the dynamic detector the same shifts act through $F'$ and the susceptibility, without a sign rule. The state dependenceis }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, without a PEIX-based sign reversal: voltage excursions of order
$I_{\rm ac}/(\omega\tau_m)$ generate a positive input shift through $v^2$.
A shorter membrane time can therefore change both the magnitude and sign
of the mean response at a given physical carrier frequency.
}

\DIFadd{This fast-carrier attenuation includes membrane access. Normalizing by the
squared voltage excursion leaves a coefficient tending to $F'/2$.
For two nearby current tones of amplitudes $I_j$, voltage squaring gives a
beat source proportional to $I_1I_2/(\omega_1\omega_2\tau_m^2)$.
The dynamic rate equation further attenuates the carriers after mixing;
that carrier attenuation is not applied to the generated beat. The crossover is derived in
Appendix~\ref{app:mpr-coefficients}; fixed-voltage coefficients and carrier
exponents are supplementary (\S\ref{supp:dynamic-details}).
}

\DIFadd{In this cellular realization of quadratic detection, }\DIFaddend the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{falsifiable handle; the sign reversal is specific
to the memoryless model}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{unit coefficient of $v^2$ in membrane-time units
plays the role of $\Sig''/2$; $G$ includes the voltage and rate dynamics
converting that source into a rate beat. Other smooth cellular mechanisms
share the weak-input product law without sharing its coefficient, phase,
or carrier dependence}\DIFaddend .

%DIF <  fig:opp moved to Results (Theory is equations-only)
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \section{Methods}

\DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Response terminology.}}\label{sec:vocab-methods}
\DIFadd{A \emph{forced response} is the beat-driven component measured by lock-in; a purely
forced regime requires stability under stimulation. \emph{Entrainment} means
frequency and phase capture of a self-sustained oscillator \cite{pikovsky2001}.
Our frequency-dominance screen does not establish that capture. \emph{Gating}
means modulation of a persisting rhythm's amplitude \cite{canolty2010,tort2010};
a rate fold alone does not establish it. Detailed criteria are in the supplement
(\S\ref{sec:vocab}).
}

\DIFaddend \subsection{Stimulation waveforms and matched-source controls}\label{sec:waveforms}
Dense perturbative sweeps use the
three-line waveform
\begin{equation}
s(t)=\varepsilon\,[\,1+m\cos\Omega t\,]\cos\omega_c t,\qquad \omega_c=2\pi f_c ,
\label{eq:amsur}
\end{equation}
with dimensionless modulation depth $m$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, because it parameterizes a sweep }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. This AM surrogate parameterizes dense
resonance and coupling sweeps }\DIFaddend by one amplitude. It \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{is not
the physical signal: it carries a line at $\omega_c$ that the two-tone sum lacks. The two
agree at $\Omega$ to leading order (the surrogate gives
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{contains a central carrier
line absent from genuine TI, but matches its leading beat source:
}\DIFaddend $Y_\Omega=\half\Sig''\varepsilon^2m$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{) and are \emph{not} equivalent beyond it}\DIFdelend . For matched genuine tones\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathrm{LPF}[s^2]=\varepsilon^2(1+\cos\Omega t)$,
whereas }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{,
$\mathrm{LPF}[s^2]=\varepsilon^2(1+\cos\Omega t)$; }\DIFaddend for the $m=1$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{AM surrogate}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{surrogate,
}\DIFaddend $\mathrm{LPF}[s^2]=\varepsilon^2(\tfrac34+\cos\Omega t+\tfrac14\cos2\Omega t)$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{: the same
$\Omega$ amplitude, but a different DC term and a spurious }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{.
Thus the mean source differs, and the surrogate adds a }\DIFaddend second harmonic at
$25\%$ of the fundamental. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Genuine two tones are used }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{We use genuine tones }\DIFaddend for the demodulation\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{demonstration
(Fig.~\ref{fig:demod}),
the curvatureand }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{,
curvature, }\DIFaddend square-law\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{controls (Fig.~\ref{fig:opp}), the
laminarmaps (Figs.~\ref{fig:lanmm_p2}, ~\ref{fig:lanmm_p1}) and the kHz demonstration.
The AM surrogate is retained for dense resonance and coupling sweeps;
Fig.~\ref{fig:opp}d verifies open-loop agreement at $\Omega$ at one operating point.
That agreement does not extend to the mean shift or second harmonic, the latter
being a diagnostic of transduction class (\S\ref{sec:transduction}). We therefore
repeat the mean-shift-dependent comparisons with two tones: the timing-versus-rate
figure uses them, as do controls of the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, laminar, and kHz demonstrations, and wherever mean shifts
or harmonics enter the comparison: timing versus rate and the }\DIFaddend TI/tACS \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{ratios }\DIFdelend and
NMM2 endpoint \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{controls. Open-loop agreement at the beat is checked at one
operating point; it does not imply equal driven operating states}\DIFaddend .
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The corresponding Results report both waveforms, whose different DC terms can
shift the recurrent operating state.
}\DIFdelend

\subsection{Amplitude scaling and harmonic measurements}\label{sec:transduction-methods}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{These measurements distinguish quadratic detection from envelope following,
which predict different dose maps and cannot be identified from a beat response
at one amplitude. With }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{At }\DIFaddend fixed coupling and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{a linear downstream response, an
amplitude sweep tests whether the generated beat scales as $\varepsilon^2$ or
$\varepsilon$. The harmonic test compares the response at the second
harmonic ,
$2\Omega$, with the fundamental at $\Omega$. After correcting for network gain,
their ratio tends to zero for weak quadratic detection but to $1/5$ for ideal envelope following.
Together these
measurements test the smooth-transfer assumption of \S\ref{sec:coupling}
using the signatures derived in \S\ref{sec:transduction}.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{operating state, we measure the fundamental and second
harmonic across an amplitude sweep to distinguish linear from quadratic scaling.
}\DIFaddend

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The response at the envelope frequency is read out with a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{A }\DIFaddend software lock-in \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{amplifier: multiplying }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{projects }\DIFaddend $v(t)$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{by }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{onto }\DIFaddend sine and cosine at $\Omega$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{and averaging estimates
the component at that frequency.
A }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{.
The }\DIFaddend finite window suppresses\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{neighboring frequencies
without eliminating them.
With a Hann window, the in-phase ($L_{\cos}$) and quadrature ($L_{\sin}$)
components are
}\begin{displaymath}
\DIFdel{\begin{gathered}
L_{\cos}=\frac{2}{\sum_k w_k}\sum_k w_k\,(v_k-\bar v)\cos\Omega t_k,\quad
L_{\sin}=\frac{2}{\sum_k w_k}\sum_k w_k\,(v_k-\bar v)\sin\Omega t_k,\\
A_\Omega=\sqrt{L_{\cos}^2+L_{\sin}^2},
\end{gathered}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{with sample index $k$, sample }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, but does not eliminate, neighboring frequencies.
For Hann weights $w_k$, sample }\DIFaddend times $t_k$, voltages $v_k=v(t_k)$, and
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Hann weights $w_k$. The windowed mean is
$\bar v=\sum_k w_k v_k/\sum_k w_k$. For a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{weighted mean $\bar v=\sum_k w_kv_k/\sum_k w_k$, the quadratures are
}\begin{equation}
\DIFadd{\begin{gathered}
L_{\cos}=\frac{2}{\sum_k w_k}\sum_k w_k\,(v_k-\bar v)\cos\Omega t_k,\quad
L_{\sin}=\frac{2}{\sum_k w_k}\sum_k w_k\,(v_k-\bar v)\sin\Omega t_k,\\
A_\Omega=\sqrt{L_{\cos}^2+L_{\sin}^2},
\end{gathered}
\label{eq:lockin}
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{where $k$ indexes samples. A }\DIFaddend test tone
$v=A_{\rm test}\cos(\Omega t+\theta_0)$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, }\DIFdelend with amplitude $A_{\rm test}$
and phase $\theta_0$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, the long-window limits
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{gives
}\DIFaddend $(L_{\cos},L_{\sin})\to A_{\rm test}(\cos\theta_0,-\sin\theta_0)$
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{give
$A_\Omega\to A_{\rm test}$: the normalization returns the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{over long windows. Thus $A_\Omega$ returns }\DIFaddend physical amplitude, twice the
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{magnitude of the windowed Fourier coefficient.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Fourier-coefficient magnitude. Mean subtraction suppresses leakage from
the larger baseline (supplement, \S\ref{supp:lockin}).
}

\ifjne\else
\subsection{\DIFadd{Lock-in leakage control}}\label{supp:lockin}
\DIFaddend Out-of-band components can leak into the estimate, particularly the DC level of the JR voltage readout ($\bar v\!\sim\!8$~mV, far
larger than the demodulated signal). The finite-window projection of a constant onto the quadrature references
need not vanish, so we subtract $\bar v$ explicitly; this makes the metric
accurate down to the $10^{-8}$ level needed for the JR kHz
analysis\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(\S\ref{sec:khz})}\DIFdelend .

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The log--log slope of the envelope-locked response
against field amplitude separates the linear and quadratic laws of
\S\ref{sec:transduction} without requiring absolute
calibration; the $\varepsilon^2$ control of \S\ref{sec:results-curvature} is that
measurement, performed in the model.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \fi
\DIFaddend

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{To measure that harmonic ratio, apply Eq.~\eqref{eq:lockin} at $\Omega$ and $2\Omega$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{A detector comparison requires both harmonics }\DIFaddend over the same settled window\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. Their amplitude ratio estimates $\mathcal H_{\rm meas}$;
correcting for }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{,
with their amplitude ratio corrected by }\DIFaddend $|G(2\Omega)/G(\Omega)|$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{gives $\mathcal H_{\rm det}$.
Inferring }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{.
Estimating }\DIFaddend a mixture coefficient through Eq.~\eqref{eq:rdet} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{additionally requires the mixed
family
$T(\mathcal A)=c_1\mathcal A+c_2\mathcal A^2$ and a known }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{assumes the mixed
detector family and its }\DIFaddend sign branch.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{A threshold response can give a similar ratio at one amplitude, so the amplitude sweep must also be checked.At resonance ($\Omega=\omega_0$)with
$\mathcal{Q}=10$, a detector ratio of $20\%$ reads as $0.7\%$, making an envelope
follower appear square-law. The test therefore requires an
\emph{off-resonance} drive:
at $\Delta f\simeq f_0/4$ both lines sit on the flat part of $G$,
where
$|G(2\Omega)/G(\Omega)|\approx1.22$--$1.25$ across $\mathcal{Q}=3$--$30$, or with $G$
measured independently by a weak sweep of direct low-frequency stimulation (transcranial
alternating-current stimulation,tACS) and
deconvolved. }\DIFdelend

\subsection{Head model and field computation}\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \label{sec:fem-methods}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The realistic-head comparison of \S\ref{sec:fem-results} uses }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \label{sec:fem-methods}
\ifjne
\DIFadd{We compute maps in IXI002, an open-access T1-weighted MRI of a 35-year-old
woman, using the hippocampal montage of Violante et al.\ \cite{violante2023}.
The six-tissue SimNIBS conductor is quasi-static with no kHz dispersion correction
\cite{salvador2025}. Pair fields are solved once and scaled to peak currents
summing to $4$~mA: $2+2$~mA for 1:1 and $1+3$~mA for 1:3. The posterior share
is swept from $0.1$ to $0.9$. Conductivities, electrodes, solver settings,
and sensitivity protocols are in the supplement (\S\ref{supp:fem-protocol}).
}

\DIFadd{Local polarization is $\varepsilon_j=\lambda_0 I_j^{\rm pair}\mathbf F_j\cdot\mathbf n$
with uniform $\lambda_0=0.5$~mV per V/m \cite{radman2009} and field per unit
current $\mathbf F_j$. This coupling sets absolute amplitudes but cancels from
normalized maps, focus positions, and target-to-reference ratios. It is an
assumed field-to-polarization conversion, not a measured AIS transfer.
}

\DIFadd{At each node, $\mathbf n$ maximizes the envelope at 1:1 and is then held fixed
for every ratio and every map. This geometric direction need not match a cell's
orientation. Radial, laminar, and isotropically averaged projections provide
sensitivity comparisons (supplement, \S\ref{supp:fem-sensitivities} and \S\ref{supp:fem-orientation}); the latter averages the signed beat before taking its magnitude,
allowing cancellation across orientations.
}

\DIFadd{The target is the left hippocampus segmented with FreeSurfer (1,568 gray- and
white-matter nodes). The reference comprises 273,544 gray-matter nodes outside
that ROI, including other deep gray matter. The target-to-reference contrast
is the ROI 95th percentile divided by the reference 95th percentile; the
mean-based companion replaces the numerator by the ROI mean. Nodes receive
equal weight. Square roots of second-order maps provide degree-matched geometric
contrasts; the raw maps remain the detector predictions before gain.
}

\DIFadd{The focus is the centroid of the top 10\% of ROI nodes ranked by each map.
Displacement is measured along the hippocampal long axis, with anterior positive.
Sensitivity checks vary montage, atlas label, gray/white-matter inclusion,
focus fraction, and orientation. These geometric maps precede detection and
recurrence: $Y_{\rm DC}=\Sig''(v^*)\mathcal I/2$ is a signed rescaling only
for uniform curvature. The head model does not specify the operating states.
}

\else
\label{supp:fem-protocol}
\DIFadd{To compare the spatial predictions on the same anatomy, we compute all maps
in }\DIFaddend one open-access subject,
IXI002 of the IXI dataset\footnote{\url{https://brain-development.org/ixi-dataset/}}
(T1-weighted MRI; female, 35 years). The T1 image was segmented into six tissues
(scalp, skull, cerebrospinal fluid, gray matter, white matter and eyes) and meshed with
SimNIBS~3.2.3 \texttt{headreco} \cite{nielsen2018}, with its CAT12 option
\cite{gaser2024}. Tissue conductivities were 0.33 (scalp), 0.008 (skull), 1.79
(cerebrospinal fluid), 0.40 (gray matter), 0.15 (white matter) and 0.33~S/m (eyes)
\cite{salvador2025}. These are low-frequency values; no dispersion correction was
applied at the kHz carriers. The volume conductor is linear and quasi-static, so the
carrier frequencies do not enter the maps.

The electrodes follow the published hippocampal montage of Violante et al.\
\cite{violante2023}, placed by its recipe on the subject's registered 10--10
positions. Pair~1 (anterior) joins e1, 25~mm from T9 toward FT9, to e2, 10~mm from AF8
toward Fp2. Pair~2 (posterior) joins e3, 25~mm from T9 toward TP9, to e4, 10~mm from P8
toward PO8. Each electrode is a 20-mm disc of 3~mm of gel (4.0~S/m) under 2~mm of metal
(10~S/m), meshed into the head model with SimNIBS~3.2.3; the resulting mesh has
$3.64\times10^6$ linear tetrahedra. As a montage sensitivity, the 10--10 rendering of
this montage, FT7--F8 (anterior) and TP7--P8 (posterior), which Yatsuda et al.\
\cite{yatsuda2024} used as their starting montage, was meshed and solved in the same
way. Each montage has its own mesh, carrying only its own four electrodes.

Each pair was solved once at 1~A. The potential $\phi_j$ (V) satisfies
$\nabla\!\cdot\!(\sigma_{\rm cond}\nabla\phi_j)=0$, where
$\sigma_{\rm cond}$ is tissue conductivity (S/m). Current enters and leaves through each
electrode's metal contact as a Neumann condition, distributed in proportion to nodal
area; the rest of the scalp carries no normal current, and one pinned node fixes the
gauge. The linear finite-element system was assembled and solved with Neuroelectrics'
temporal-interference library, by conjugate gradients with algebraic-multigrid
preconditioning, to a relative residual of $10^{-8}$. The field
$-\nabla\phi_j$ was recovered at gray- and white-matter
nodes by superconvergent patch recovery \cite{zienkiewicz1992}, separately within each
compartment (446{,}877 node--compartment pairs). Dividing these fields by the solve
current gives the field-per-current vectors $\mathbf F_j$, expressed in
(V/m)/mA to match $I_j^{\rm pair}$. Every current condition is a scaling of these two fields.
With peak pair currents $I_1^{\rm pair}$ (anterior) and
$I_2^{\rm pair}$ (posterior) summing to 4~mA, the ratio 1:1 is $2+2$~mA and 1:3 is
1~mA anterior plus 3~mA posterior, the two conditions of Violante et al. The ratio sweep
sets the posterior share $I_2^{\rm pair}/(I_1^{\rm pair}+I_2^{\rm pair})$ from 0.1 to
0.9 in steps of 0.025.

The field-to-polarization conversion follows \S\ref{sec:envelope}:
$\varepsilon_j(\mathbf x)=I_j^{\rm pair}\mathcal P_j(\mathbf x)$, with
$\mathcal P_j=\lambda_0\,\mathbf F_j\!\cdot\!\mathbf n$, one uniform
static coupling $\lambda_0=0.5$~mV per V/m \cite{radman2009}, and
$\lambda_f\approx\lambda_0$. A uniform coupling multiplies the envelope map by
$\lambda_0$ and the two second-order maps by $\lambda_0^2$. Target-to-reference ratios,
normalized maps and focus positions do not depend on it. Hippocampal couplings of
$0.12$--$0.18$~mV per V/m \cite{bikson2004,deans2007} would scale the absolute values
by $0.24$--$0.36$ and $0.06$--$0.13$, respectively.

The \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{projection direction }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{detector compartment fixes the projection direction. A laminar direction for a pyramidal
axon initial segment, a local axon direction for a node, or an orientation
distribution for terminals defines a different access model. Isotropic averaging
is one possible distribution, not an anatomical consequence of choosing terminals.
Maximizing a geometric map over orientation gives an upper bound for that map
at each location; it does not identify the detector's orientation.
}

\DIFadd{The projection direction }\DIFaddend $\mathbf n(\mathbf x)=\mathbf n^*(\mathbf x)$ is the envelope-maximizing direction at
the reference ratio 1:1: the closed-form maximizer, over unit vectors, of
$2\min(|\mathbf F_1\!\cdot\!\mathbf n|,|\mathbf F_2\!\cdot\!\mathbf n|)$
\cite{grossman2017}. It is chosen once at each node and then held fixed for 1:1, 1:3
and every ratio of the sweep, and all three maps use it. Along it, the 1:1 envelope map
equals its maximum over orientation. At any other ratio it is the envelope seen by an
element with that fixed orientation, which can be below that ratio's own maximum. As an
orientation sensitivity, every statistic was repeated along a second fixed field, the
radial direction from the brain centroid. The isotropic comparison averages the signed
mixing term over uniformly distributed orientations before its magnitude is taken:
$\langle\varepsilon_1\varepsilon_2\rangle_{\mathbf n}
=\lambda_0^2I_1^{\rm pair}I_2^{\rm pair}\,\mathbf F_1\!\cdot\!\mathbf F_2/3$, so the
isotropic beat excursion is
$\tfrac23\lambda_0^2I_1^{\rm pair}I_2^{\rm pair}|\mathbf F_1\!\cdot\!\mathbf F_2|$.
Elements whose beats have opposite phase cancel in this average.

The target is the left hippocampus of a FreeSurfer~6.0.0 \texttt{recon-all}
segmentation of the same T1 image (\texttt{aseg} label 17, 3903~mm$^3$)
\cite{fischl2002}. The region of interest (ROI) is the 1{,}568 gray- and white-matter
nodes inside it. The reference is the 273{,}544 gray-matter nodes outside the
ROI, including other deep gray matter. It \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{serves as a cortical-reference proxy}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{is a gray-matter reference, not a cortex-only contrast}\DIFaddend .
The mesh and the segmentation share one image frame; tissue-volume, centroid and
white-matter checks confirmed their alignment. As a label sensitivity, the
Neuromorphometrics atlas hippocampus, warped into the subject with SimNIBS~3.2.3
\texttt{mni2subject} and the subject's nonlinear MNI transform (1{,}345 nodes; Dice 0.72
with the FreeSurfer label), replaced the ROI. As a tissue sensitivity, the ROI was
restricted to its gray-matter nodes (1{,}174 with the FreeSurfer label, 1{,}013 with
the atlas label).

The target-to-reference ratio is the ROI 95th percentile divided by the reference
95th percentile; its mean-based companion divides the ROI mean by the same
reference percentile. All percentiles and centroids weight nodes equally.
The quadratic excursion and $\mathcal I$ are second order in the field, whereas
the envelope excursion is first order. To separate this difference in degree
from the spatial weighting, each second-order statistic is also given
degree-matched, computed on the square root of the map. This is a geometric
comparison; the raw maps remain the respective detector predictions before gain.
The focus is the centroid of the top 10\% of ROI nodes,
ranked by the map's own value, with 5, 25 and 50\% as a sensitivity. Ranking makes the
focus exactly invariant to a uniform positive factor. Its displacement from 1:1 is
measured along the hippocampal long axis, the first principal axis of the ROI nodes,
with anterior positive. Field projections still use $\mathbf n^*$, independently
of this reporting axis. The 1:1 to 1:3 displacement was computed for every combination
of montage, label, ROI tissue definition and focus fraction (32 per map), each montage
along its own 1:1 direction.

No signed DC-source or mean-response map is computed. For a spatially uniform detector
coefficient, $Y_{\rm DC}=\tfrac12\Sig''(v^*)\mathcal I$ (\S\ref{sec:dc}) is
$\mathcal I$ rescaled, with the sign of $\Sig''(v^*)$. A nonuniform coefficient would
require an operating-point map, which a head model does not provide, and a mean
response would additionally require a circuit model. In particular,
$\Sig''(v^*)$ is curvature at the population model's operating point, not a
measurement of the transfer at the axon initial segment. Assigning that
coefficient to an anatomical detector requires a calibrated access and
input--output model.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The script
\texttt{code/fem\_quadmaps.py} computes every map and number in
\S\ref{sec:fem-results} from the stored fields, and \texttt{code/make\_fem\_fig.py}
draws Fig.~\ref{fig:fem}; \texttt{docs/fem\_ixi002\_violante.md} lists every setting.
}\DIFdelend

\DIFaddbegin \fi

\DIFaddend \subsection{Single-column Jansen--Rit: integration, lock-in, and controls}\label{sec:jr-methods}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{All symbols and units are collected in Table~\ref{tab:notation}
(App.~\ref{app:notation}). }\DIFdelend We integrate the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{single-column JR model of \S\ref{sec:nmm}
(App.~\ref{app:models}, Eqs.~\eqref{eq:jr0} and Table~\ref{tab:params}) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{JR column }\DIFaddend as six first-order \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{ODEs}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{ordinary differential equations}\DIFaddend . The TI field enters only the pyramidal output sigmoid, Eq.~\eqref{eq:jr0},
as $s(t)$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{; the read-out }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, while the readout }\DIFaddend $v=y_1-y_2$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{is \emph{not} fed the field directly, so any
power at $\Omega$ in $v$ is produced by }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{contains only synaptic potentials.
A beat in this readout must therefore have passed through }\DIFaddend the nonlinearity
and the recurrent loop\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{,
not by trivial pass-through}\DIFdelend . Unless stated otherwise $f_c=100$~Hz and $m=1$.

\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Integration uses fixed-step fourth-order Runge--Kutta (RK4), with time steps
chosen for each carrier band. Halving each step changes the reported lock-in
amplitudes by less than $1\%$. The resonance sweep settles for $10$~s and measures
for $20$~s; run-specific steps and windows are listed in the supplement
(\S\ref{supp:jr-runs}). The quoted $10$~kHz attenuation is analytic, not simulated.
}\ifjne\else
\subsection{\DIFadd{JR integration and measurement windows}}\label{supp:jr-runs}
\DIFaddend We integrate with a fixed-step fourth-order Runge--Kutta (RK4) scheme,
$\Delta t=2\times10^{-4}$~s (50 samples per carrier cycle at $100$~Hz; reduced to
$10^{-4}$~s for carrier sweeps up to $300$~Hz). The kHz-carrier runs of
Fig.~\ref{fig:khz_direct} use $\Delta t=2\times10^{-5}$~s for the carrier sweep to
$4$~kHz (25 samples per carrier cycle at $2$~kHz, 12.5 at $4$~kHz) and
$1.5\times10^{-5}$~s for the $2$~kHz output spectrum. Halving each step changes the
reported lock-in amplitudes by less than $1\%$. The $10$~kHz figures quoted in
\S\ref{sec:khz} are analytic evaluations of Eq.~\eqref{eq:khz}, not simulations.

The resonance sweep \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{in Fig.~\ref{fig:res} }\DIFdelend uses $10$~s of settling and a $20$~s
measurement window. \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The kHz carrier sweep uses $12$~s of settling and
a $3$~s measurement window. }\DIFaddend Long windows reduce transients from weakly damped modes and
average beating when autonomous activity persists. Other runs use the windows
specified in their scripts.
The two-tone $2$~kHz example (Fig.~\ref{fig:khz_twotone}) uses $p=400$~s$^{-1}$,
$C=135$, and $0.5$~mV per tone with $\tau=0.2$~ms. From a zero initial state,
it settles for $4$~s and records $4$~s at $\Delta t=1.5\times10^{-5}$~s,
sampling before each RK4 step. Its amplitude spectra use plain-mean subtraction
and a Hann window. Parameter sweeps \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{are vectorized: the state is an
$(N_{\rm grid},6)$ array, where }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{advance all }\DIFaddend $N_{\rm grid}$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{counts the parameter combinations advanced simultaneously
, so a full
resonance map runs in seconds}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{combinations simultaneously
in a vectorized $(N_{\rm grid},6)$ state array}\DIFaddend .

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The full bifurcation diagram (Fig.~\ref{fig:bif}) is computed by numerical continuation of
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \fi

\DIFadd{Numerical continuation gives }\DIFaddend the fixed-point and limit-cycle branches \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. We independently locate the operating
supercritical Hopf by solving , for each $p$, the self-consistent scalar equation for the
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{in
Fig.~\ref{fig:bif}. Independently solving the }\DIFaddend resting fixed point \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$v=y_1-y_2$, forming the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and tracking
the leading pair of its }\DIFaddend $6\times6$ Jacobian \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{numerically and
diagonalizing it: the Hopf is the $p$ at
which the leading eigenvalue pair crosses the
imaginary axis with $\mathrm{Im}\,\mu_+$ in the alpha band. This gives
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{locates the upper Hopf at
}\DIFaddend $p_H\approx315.70$~s$^{-1}$ and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$f_0=\mathrm{Im}\,\mu_+/2\pi\approx11.16$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$f_0\approx11.16$}\DIFaddend ~Hz, consistent with
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the continuation and the JR analysis of \cite{grimbert2006}. The $11.1$~Hz demonstration beat is near this resonance. }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\cite{grimbert2006}. }\DIFaddend For $p>p_H$ the fixed point is \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{a stablefocus.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{stable; the $11.1$~Hz
example drives it near resonance.
}

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{We compare the \emph{open-loop detector}, the firing-rate sigmoid at a fixed operating point }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The open-loop sigmoid at fixed }\DIFaddend $v^*$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{without recurrence, with the \emph{closed-loop} column, the full recurrent
Jansen--Rit with its feedback. The open-loop detector passes the two-tone field through
$\Sig$ at $v^*$ and applies Eq.~\eqref{eq:lockin}, isolating the pure demodulation law
(Figs.~\ref{fig:carrier},~\ref{fig:opp},~\ref{fig:khz})}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{isolates detection}\DIFaddend ; its signed \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{in-phase
component $L_{\cos}$ verifies }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{lock-in
tests }\DIFaddend the curvature law\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, including its sign. The \emph{bifurcation
diagram} records the field-free min/max of $v$ vs.\ $p$, each run initialized at
its fixed point plus a small kick. The \emph{two-dimensional map} evaluates
$A_\Omega$ over
an ($\Omega,p$) grid. We verify the mechanism three ways: the
square law ($A_\Omega$ vs.\ $\varepsilon$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. The closed-loop column measures recurrent gain over
an $(\Omega,p)$ grid. Controls test quadratic amplitude scaling }\DIFaddend on a well-damped
focus, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{expecting a
log--log slope of $2$); a linearization control (replacing $\Sig$ in
the pyramidal sigmoid of App.
~\ref{app:models}, Eq.
~\eqref{eq:jr0}, by its tangent at $v^*$, which must abolish the $\Omega$
response); and a check of $\Sig',\Sig''$ against finite differences. Code and reproduction limits are listed under \emph{Code and data availability}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{abolition of the beat when the pyramidal sigmoid is replaced by its
tangent, and agreement of $\Sig'$ and $\Sig''$ with finite differences.
Field-free trajectories initialized near the fixed point check the continuation.
}

\DIFadd{The coupling sweep holds detection fixed: for each $C$, we solve for the external
drive $p(C)$ that holds the field-free voltage at $v^*=8.30$~mV, then obtain damping and modal frequency
from the Jacobian. This fixes the background curvature without canceling
stimulation-induced mean shifts.
}

\DIFadd{We use the AM surrogate for the gain sweep and genuine two tones to compare
modulation and mean of the same pyramidal rate
$r_P=\Sig(v+s)$. The tACS control uses a direct sinusoid at the same beat
frequency and reads the same voltage lock-in as TI. Further controls match
tACS to TI's generated beat source and repeat the comparison with longer
settling and measurement windows. Waveforms, amplitudes, and run windows
are recorded together in the supplement}\DIFaddend .

\subsection{Exact next-generation mean field (NMM2 PING)}\label{sec:nmm2-methods}
The \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{NMM2 PING}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{pyramidal--interneuron network gamma (PING) }\DIFaddend circuit couples excitatory
and inhibitory MPR populations through second-order synapses
\cite{montbrio2015,ruffini2021nmm2,clusella2023}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{.
We use }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, using }\DIFaddend the Rosetta Stone
formulation \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\cite{ruffini2025rosetta}, which places this
derived mean field on the same footing as JR and LaNMM (\S\ref{sec:nmm})}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{shared with JR and laminar models \cite{ruffini2025rosetta}}\DIFaddend .
For population $X\in\{E,I\}$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(excitatory or inhibitory)}\DIFdelend , $r_X$ is \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the physical firing }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{physical }\DIFaddend rate (ms$^{-1}$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{in the integration}\DIFdelend ),
$v_X$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the dimensionless mean QIF
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{dimensionless mean }\DIFaddend voltage, and $\tau_X$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the }\DIFdelend membrane time (ms).
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Dots denote }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{With dots denoting }\DIFaddend physical-time derivatives\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{:
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{,
}\DIFaddend \begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\tau_E\dot r_E&=\Delta/(\pi\tau_E)+2r_Ev_E, &\quad
\tau_E\dot v_E&=v_E^2+\bar\eta-(\pi\tau_E r_E)^2+\tau_E J\,(r_E^{\rm syn}-r_I^{\rm syn})+I_E(t),\\[2pt]
\tau_I\dot r_I&=\Delta/(\pi\tau_I)+2r_Iv_I, &\quad
\tau_I\dot v_I&=v_I^2+\bar\eta-(\pi\tau_I r_I)^2+\tau_I J\,(r_E^{\rm syn}-2r_I^{\rm syn}),
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:nmm2-ping}
\end{equation}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{with second-order synaptic activations }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The synaptic rates }\DIFaddend $r_E^{\rm syn}=\hat K_a[r_E]$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(excitatory, time constant
$\tau_a$) }\DIFdelend and
$r_I^{\rm syn}=\hat K_g[r_I]$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(inhibitory, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, with times $\tau_a$ and }\DIFaddend $\tau_g$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{),
each obeying the same
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{,
obey the }\DIFaddend critically damped operator
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{as the JR synapse, }\DIFdelend $\tau_s^2\ddot r_{\rm syn}+2\tau_s\dot r_{\rm syn}+r_{\rm syn}=r$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{; here }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{.
Here }\DIFaddend $J$ is \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the dimensionless global synaptic gainand the relative }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{dimensionless global gain; }\DIFaddend weights
$(w_{EE},w_{EI},w_{IE},w_{II})=(1,1,1,2)$ use target\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$X$ and source $Y$ in $w_{XY}$ and set the E and I rows: the $E$ membrane sees $J\,(r_E^{\rm syn}-r_I^{\rm syn})$ and the $I$ membrane
$J\,(r_E^{\rm syn}-2r_I^{\rm syn})$,
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{/source indices,
with doubled inhibitory self-coupling as }\DIFaddend the only asymmetry\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{being the doubled $I\!\to\!I$ weight $w_{II}=2$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{.
In the schematic, $\mathcal U_j(t)$ denotes neuron $j$'s total synaptic
and field input, and $J^*$ the coupling-induced Hopf}\DIFaddend .

The \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{common background drive }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{background }\DIFaddend $\bar\eta$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{is the bifurcation parameter: raising it
past }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{controls }\DIFaddend a supercritical Hopf \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{at $\bar\eta_H=1.110$ opens a gamma
limit cycle(the gamma
counterpart of the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{opening a gamma
cycle, analogous to the }\DIFaddend JR alpha Hopf\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{). The applied }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. The }\DIFaddend field enters the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{excitatory voltage equation as a current-like forcing, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{E-voltage
equation as }\DIFaddend $I_E(t)=\lambda_I E(t)$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{in the convention of the exact mean field
\cite{ruffini2021nmm2}, with instantaneous field-to-current }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\cite{ruffini2021nmm2}.
The instantaneous }\DIFaddend coupling $\lambda_I$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, as two genuine }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{has units of normalized drive per V/m;
two }\DIFaddend tones at $f_c\mp\Delta f/2$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(carrier $f_c=300$~Hz, envelope $\Delta f$ swept through the gamma band)
of current }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{have }\DIFaddend amplitude $A_f$ in the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{dimensionless MPR drive units , the same
normalization }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{same
dimensionless drive units }\DIFaddend as $\bar\eta$. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Thus $\lambda_I$ is measured in normalized drive per V/m. The E membrane $\tau_E\dot v_E$
integrates this forcing into the carrier-frequency }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{We set $f_c=300$~Hz and sweep
$\Delta f$ through gamma. The membrane integrates each tone into an
}\DIFaddend excursion $A_f/(\omega\tau_E)$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{that the
exact quadratic }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, which }\DIFaddend $v_E^2$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{term rectifies, with no postulated sigmoid; the PING as run
therefore computes membrane access with }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{rectifies: access uses
}\DIFaddend $\tau_m=\tau_E$\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, without an assumed sigmoid}\DIFaddend .

We integrate with fixed-step RK4 ($\Delta t=0.05$~ms) and read out the lock-in
(Eq.~\eqref{eq:lockin}) of $r_E$ at $\Delta f$ over the $(\Delta f, \bar\eta)$ plane.
Parameters (Rosetta Stone PING \cite{ruffini2025rosetta}): $\Delta=1$, global coupling
$J=15$, $\tau_E=15$, $\tau_I=7.5$, $\tau_a=10$, $\tau_g=2.5$~ms, with $\bar\eta$ swept
through the supercritical Hopf at $\bar\eta_H=1.110$ ($f_0=54.0$~Hz), located by bisection
on the leading eigenvalue of the mean-field Jacobian. The simulations retain these
finite membrane times; Eq.~\eqref{eq:slow_synapse} characterizes the fast-membrane limit.

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{As in JR, either background drive or coupling can bring the circuit to its Hopf.
}\DIFdelend The resonance map \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{sweeps }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{varies }\DIFaddend $\bar\eta$ (Fig.~\ref{fig:nmm2_map}); the
coupling sweep \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{,
called the J-curve, }\DIFdelend varies $J$ at fixed $\bar\eta$ toward $J^*$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, located by bisection on the leading
Jacobian eigenvalue, and reads the
lock-in peak of $r_E$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and measures the
peak rate lock-in }\DIFaddend over $\Delta f$ (Fig.~\ref{fig:nmm2_jcurve}).
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Unlike the JR J-curve, this }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The $J^*$ Hopf is located by the same eigenvalue bisection.
Unlike JR, the PING coupling }\DIFaddend sweep does not pin the operating point. At
$\bar\eta=0$, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{we track the eigenpair that reaches the Hopf by
continuation and define $\gamma$ from that critical pair , even where }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{continuation tracks the critical pair defining $\gamma$,
even when }\DIFaddend another mode dominates\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the response}\DIFdelend . The linear reference includes the
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{operating-state
dependence of the carrier response and the recurrent transfer. We compute its
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{state-dependent source and full transfer; grids, amplitude checks, and
normalizations are supplementary (\S\ref{supp:ping-runs}).
}

\subsection{\DIFadd{A cutoff-free theta-neuron check}}\label{supp:theta-pilot}
\DIFadd{The change of variables $V_j=\tan(\theta_j/2)$ converts the QIF equation to
}\[
\DIFadd{\tau_X\dot\theta_j=(1-\cos\theta_j)
 +(1+\cos\theta_j)(\eta_j+U_X(t)),
}\]

the finite voltage cutoff and reset. We linearly interpolate the crossing
time for phase statistics. Empirical spike counts still drive the original
second-order synapses. Excitability quantiles, initial voltage, coupling,
carrier, and readouts are unchanged.

doubling, and window doubling before extending this solver to carrier controls.

stays at $2.8$--$2.9\%$.

The three cutoff results are compatible with an inverse-cutoff correction:
a fit of $d_\infty-c/V_{\rm peak}$ }\DIFaddend gives \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$0.083$ and halving the time step gives $0.093$. The response is therefore
sensitive to the finite spike cutoff. Halving the step at cutoffs $200$ and

At the plotted cutoff, the pooled }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$d_\infty=0.156$, with residuals
of about $0.002$. This agreement supports a cutoff contribution but does not

\DIFadd{Pooled }\DIFaddend spike-phase \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{vector strength is $0.046$;
median }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{vector strength rises from $0.002$ without stimulation
to $0.068$--$0.077$ in the driven runs. Median }\DIFaddend per-neuron \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{phase consistency remains negative in field-off and field-on
windows at all three cutoffs.
In the $A_f=35$ convergence checks, halving the time step changes the lock-in and the pooled vector
strength by under $1\%$; doubling the spike-detection threshold changes the lock-in by about $1\%$ and the mean rate by $2\%$, but the pooled vector strength at
$\bar\eta=0.85$ by about $45\%$, so phase concentration, unlike rate modulation, is sensitive to
how spike times are defined.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{phase consistency
remains negative, from $-0.015$ to $-0.005$, and supplies no evidence for
strong individual phase locking. The finite network therefore supports the
presence of population-rate modulation; its quantitative weak-input depth
and agreement with the mean field remain unresolved.
}\DIFaddend

each read out by the

That comparison uses $18$~s settling and a $20$~s window for each waveform.

$v^*=8.30$~mV, TI surrogate amplitude $0.3$~mV with $m=1$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$55$~Hz and $34$ at $42$~Hz ($800$ }\DIFaddend and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$f_c=100$~Hz, and tACS amplitude $0.1$~mV,

}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\subsection{\DIFdel{Vocabulary: forced response, entrainment, and gating}}%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{sec:vocab-methods}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{\emph{Forced response} denotes the component driven at the beat $\Delta f$,
quantified by the lock-in amplitude (Eq.
~\eqref{eq:lockin}). A purely forced regime
requires a stable operating state under stimulation; being below Hopf with the field
off is insufficient. \emph{Entrainment} is reserved for its
dynamical-systems meaning, frequency capture of a \emph{self-sustained} oscillator
\cite{pikovsky2001}; in the maps we screen for it with the
dominance criterion
$|f_{\rm out}-\Delta f|<0.3$~Hz on the dominant output frequency. This identifies
frequency dominance, not phase capture. \emph{Gating} is amplitude modulation of a persisting rhythm without frequency capture \cite{canolty2010,tort2010}. Capture and gating are dynamically different, and the same word would otherwise cover
both. The QIF rate fold measures beat-locked firing, which does not by itself
establish gating of gamma amplitude. Appendix~\ref{sec:vocab} gives the
metrics
and the frequency-dominance protocol}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$810$~ms)}\DIFaddend .

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The coupling sweeps approach the Hopf from }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{We also run both conditions at $A_f=10$ per tone with the same measurement
settings. For $\bar\eta=0.85$, separate checks double the population size,
double the measurement window, or halve the integration step; further runs raise
$V_{\rm peak}$ to $200$ and $400$ at both time steps. An equal-RMS
mean-field control replaces the two tones by one $300$~Hz carrier with peak
amplitude $\sqrt{2}A_f$, using the same duration: both inputs have mean-square
amplitude $A_f^2$ over these windows. The carrier comparison starts from the
}\DIFaddend field-free \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{stable foci ($\gamma>0$), so the
$1/\gamma$ law concerns forced susceptibility. The frequency-dominance maps on
the oscillatory side describe a different observable: their bands broaden with
drive, but an Arnold tongue describes phase capture of the autonomous limit
cycle and
requires phase tracking (\S\ref{sec:vocab}).
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{equilibrium and uses $800$~ms settling and $800$~ms measurement,
with a $3200$~ms window to check spectral leakage at the larger amplitude.
}\DIFaddend

\DIFdelbegin \section{\DIFdel{Results}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{section}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{The Jansen--Rit column and the exact next-generation mean field (NMM2)both generate a
difference-frequency component and amplify it near a Hopf bifurcation. The former uses
a memoryless sigmoid; the latter derives the nonlinearity from QIF dynamics. Their
detector coefficients differ, but both separate beat generation from recurrent gain. Coupling controls that gain at fixed generated drive in the JR column
(\S\ref{sec:jcurve}), and resonance amplifies beat-locked modulation more strongly than
the mean rate (\S\ref{sec:timing}). The TI/tACS comparison then isolates their
shared amplifier (\S\ref{sec:tacs-results}), before the
NMM2 and QIF results
connect dynamic mixing and gain to spikes and field-induced gamma.
The laminar
two-band column extends the analysis to carriers of $25$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{A longer window reduces the single carrier's apparent $55$~Hz
depth from $0.011$ to $9.2\times10^{-5}$, while TI's stays at $0.30$}\DIFaddend --\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$80$~Hz
and drives up to $3$~mV (App.
~\ref{app:lanmm}).
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$0.31$;
the former is spectral leakage.
}\DIFaddend

\DIFaddbegin \fi

\section{\DIFadd{Results}}
\DIFaddend \subsection{The \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Jansen--Rit sigmoid demodulates: an alpha line from a carrier-only input}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{JR column generates and resonantly amplifies the beat}\DIFaddend }
An input containing only the two tones $f_c\mp\Delta f/2$ produces an output
oscillation at their $11.1$~Hz beat, near the column's alpha resonance
(Fig.~\ref{fig:bif}). The alpha peak is absent from the input spectrum
(Fig.~\ref{fig:demod}).

\DIFdelbegin \subsection{\DIFdel{Near the Hopf, the demodulated beat drives a sharp resonance}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend In the stable-focus regime ($p>p_H$), the response is approximately Lorentzian
and its peak grows from $0.23$ to $0.43$ to $0.70$~mV toward the Hopf
(Fig.~\ref{fig:res}), as expected from the
$1/\gamma$ susceptibility. Without a drive the focus is silent at $\Omega$;
the nonlinearity generates that component and recurrence amplifies it.
\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The peak shifts with the driven operating state; the near-Hopf law gives
its leading gain, not its exact frequency. The coupling sweep tests that law
over a decade of damping.
}\DIFaddend On the alpha branch below $p_H$, the column free-runs at $f_0$, so the lock-in
can contain both driven and autonomous activity. The dominant output frequency
$f_{\rm out}$ then identifies a frequency-dominance region
(Fig.~\ref{fig:entrain}), without establishing phase capture.

\begin{figure}[t!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_resonance.pdf}
\caption{\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\textbf{The demodulated beat drives a resonance that sharpens toward the Hopf (Jansen--Rit).}
Fixed-point regime ($p>p_H$, stable focus): lock-in }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{\textbf{The JR beat resonance sharpens toward the Hopf.}
Stable-focus }\DIFaddendFL response at \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$\Omega$ vs.\ envelope
frequency (}\DIFdelendFL $\varepsilon=0.3$~mV\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{)}\DIFdelendFL . \emph{(a)} \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{The demodulated drive forces an approximately Lorentzian
resonance near $f_0\!\approx\!11.2$~Hz. The measured }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{Lock-in amplitude
against beat frequency; the }\DIFaddendFL finite-amplitude peak \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{shifts
}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{moves }\DIFaddendFL from $11.08$ to
$10.93$~Hz across the curves\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{as the operating state changes;
Eq}\DIFdelendFL . \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{~\eqref{eq:gain} describes its near-Hopf scaling, not its exact location.
The response carries no power at $\Omega$ in the absence of drive, so this is
forced gain. }\DIFdelendFL \emph{(b)} \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{The peak height grows }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{Peak amplitude increases }\DIFaddendFL as \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$p$
approaches }\DIFaddendFL the \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{upper }\DIFaddendFL Hopf \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{is
approached ($p\to p_H$ }\DIFdelendFL from above\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{)}\DIFdelendFL . \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{Three }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{These three }\DIFaddendFL points \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{cannot establish a power

and growth}\DIFaddendFL ; the \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{coupling sweep quantifies the }\DIFaddendFL $1/\gamma$ scaling \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{is quantified over a decade, together with }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{and

is characterized by output frequency in Fig.~\ref{fig:entrain}.}\DIFdelendFL }
\label{fig:res}
\end{figure}

}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{the sigmoid, the full JR }\DIFaddend column demodulates and resonates \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{at the recovered alpha in the fast-access case
(}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{in the assumed

Eq.~\eqref{eq:khz} predicts }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{case }\DIFaddend (Fig.~\ref{fig:khz_direct}a). \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{With two genuine }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Genuine }\DIFaddend tones centered

of the assumed access time and coupling; neither is calibrated by the simulation.

\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Figure~\ref{fig:feasibility} converts this access assumption into a source
budget. With $\varepsilon_j=\lambda_0 E_j|H_m(\omega_j)|$, the equivalent
direct beat input is $\varepsilon_{\rm eq}=Y_\Omega/\Sig'$, before recurrent
gain. At $2$~kHz, $\tau_m=0.2$~ms, $\lambda_0=0.5$~mV per V/m, and
$0.5$~V/m per tone, it has magnitude $1.36~\mu$V; the steady equivalent
is nearly identical. Reducing the assumed coupling to $0.15$ scales these
sources by $0.09$. Thus the matched-polarization $2$--$5\%$ comparison does
not carry over unchanged to matched applied fields.
}

\DIFadd{At equal fields, the $0.2$~ms point-membrane model predicts a narrowband
$9/1$~kHz source ratio of $0.020$. The limit $1/81$ applies only when
both bands lie above their access corners. A sealed long cable instead
approaches a $1/9$ ratio. These calculations locate conditional input budgets;
they do not calibrate the biological transfer.
}

\DIFaddend \subsection{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The nonlinearity is the detector: the
}%DIFDELCMD < \texorpdfstring{$\Sig''$}{sigma''} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Sigmoid }\DIFaddend curvature \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{law and its controls}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{predicts the JR beat source}\DIFaddend }\label{sec:results-curvature}
The signed open-loop \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{response tracks $\half\Sig''(v^*)\varepsilon^2 m$ across the
operating range}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{beat follows $\half\Sig''(v^*)\varepsilon^2m$}\DIFaddend , vanishing
and reversing \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{sign }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{phase }\DIFaddend at the inflection \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$v_0$
}\DIFdelend (Fig.~\ref{fig:opp})\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{---demodulation is the sigmoid-curvature term of
Eq. ~\eqref{eq:demod}, and the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. The }\DIFaddend closed-loop
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{operating points sit where $\Sig''$ is large
and negative}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{examples occupy the negative-curvature flank. At matched network
susceptibility, either flank follows the same gain law, with opposite source
phase when the curvature changes sign}\DIFaddend .

\begin{figure}[p]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_curvature_twotone.pdf}
\caption{\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\textbf{The curvature law, the square law, and the surrogate control
(Jansen--Rit, genuine two tones).} }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{\textbf{Curvature determines the JR detector.}
}\DIFaddendFL \emph{(a)} The signed open-loop \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{response tracks
$\half\Sig''(v^*)\varepsilon^2$ across the operating range (maximum deviation }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{beat follows
$\Sig''(v^*)\varepsilon^2/2$ within }\DIFaddendFL $0.9\%$\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{)}\DIFdelendFL , vanishing \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{at the inflection $v_0$ }\DIFdelendFL and reversing sign
\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{across it}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{at $v_0$}\DIFaddendFL . \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{The mechanism is
sign-agnostic: $\Sig''$ sets sign and phase while $|\Sig''|$ sets gain, so the resonance
picture is unchanged up to an overall sign in the $\Sig''>0$ lobe, which this sweep spans.
The sign flip is itself a prediction (\S\ref{sec:predictions}). }\DIFdelendFL \emph{(b)} The \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{closed-loop
}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{recurrent }\DIFaddendFL response \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{scales as $\varepsilon^2$, measured }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{has amplitude }\DIFaddendFL slope $1.99$.
\emph{(c)} \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{Replacing }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{Linearizing }\DIFaddendFL the \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{field-receiving }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{receiving }\DIFaddendFL sigmoid \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{by its tangent collapses }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{reduces }\DIFaddendFL the \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{response $634\times$}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{beat $634$-fold}\DIFaddendFL .
\emph{(d)} \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{Surrogate control: the }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{Open-loop }\DIFaddendFL two-tone/AM \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{beat }\DIFaddendFL ratio at \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$\Omega$ approaches $1$ as
$\varepsilon\!\to\!0$ (}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$v^*=8$~mV:
}\DIFaddendFL $1.0000$ at $0.02$~mV \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{, }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{and }\DIFaddendFL $0.988$ at $1$~mV\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{) for the open-loop
detector at $v^*=8$~mV}\DIFdelendFL . This checks the leading
local source\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{; it does }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{, }\DIFaddendFL not \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{validate
}\DIFdelendFL the \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{nonlinear }\DIFaddendFL recurrent \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{AM-surrogate sweeps of Fig.~\ref{fig:jcurve} }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{response }\DIFaddendFL near the Hopf.}
\label{fig:opp}
\end{figure}

In the recurrent column, the response scales quadratically at small drive
(log--log slope $1.99$, measured as in \S\ref{sec:transduction-methods}),
and linearizing the field-receiving sigmoid reduces it
from $1.34$ to $0.0021$~mV (Fig.~\ref{fig:opp}b,c). The open-loop surrogate
comparison in panel~(d) supports its use for leading-order beat sweeps, with
the waveform and near-Hopf limitations specified in \S\ref{sec:waveforms}.

\subsection{\DIFadd{Sensitivity of focus displacement}}\label{supp:fem-sensitivities}
\DIFadd{The spatial ordering in the article persists across montage, labeling, tissue,
and focus-fraction choices. Table~\ref{tab:fem-sensitivities} gives the ranges;
orientation changes absolute contrast more than the atlas label in this subject.
For the reference projection and label, the top-5--50\% focus moves
$2.2$--$4.0$~mm anteriorly for the envelope and }\DIFaddend $2.4$--$8.2$~mm \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{), toward
the pair whose current rises.
Its hippocampal mean rises by 24\%, while its ratio
changes little ($0.92$ to $0.90$).
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{posteriorly
for mean-square polarization.
}\DIFaddend

The sweep shows two kinds of steering (Fig.~\ref{fig:fem}e,f). The envelope ratio peaks
near balance ($1.24$ at a posterior share of $0.525$) and falls to $0.89$--$0.94$ at
the extremes. Its focus moves only while the two carriers are comparable inside the
hippocampus. At a posterior share of $0.6$ or more, the anterior pair is the weaker
carrier at more than 96\% of hippocampal nodes; at $0.4$ or less, the posterior pair is. The
envelope map then equals twice the weaker pair's polarization over most of the ROI,
and its measured focus changes little with further imbalance. The focus of $\mathcal I$ moves
continuously instead, from $1.5$~mm anterior at a posterior share of $0.1$ to $6.3$~mm
posterior at $0.9$, while its ratio stays between $0.79$ and $0.94$ ($0.89$--$0.97$
degree-matched). The quadratic excursion keeps one ratio and one focus across the
whole sweep.

direction $\mathbf n^*$ at $2+2$~mA.

of interest (ROI) are PC1, }\DIFaddend the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{fixed-direction quadratic map. It also
scales with $I_1^{\rm pair}I_2^{\rm pair}$, so the current ratio cannot steer it
either}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{anterior-positive long axis; PC2, lateral-positive; and
PC3$={\rm PC1}\times{\rm PC2}$, normal to its best-fit plane. Angles are
unsigned, from $0$ to $90^\circ$, and nodes receive equal weight.
Weighting by field magnitude, or envelope maximum for $\mathbf n^*$,
changes these angular medians by at most about $1.5^\circ$}\DIFaddend .

}

\subsection{\DIFadd{Continuum bounds and recovered interface jumps}}\label{supp:fem-interfaces}
\DIFadd{Median map jumps across tissue interfaces are 1.13--1.34 along the common
direction $\mathbf n^*$ (Table~\ref{tab:fem-interface-jumps}). These
projected ratios differ from the conductivity bounds on field magnitude
and maps maximized over orientation. All measurements here use IXI002,
the Violante montage, and $2+2$~mA.
}

\DIFadd{Interfaces are faces shared by tetrahedra of different tissue tags:
241,780 CSF/gray-matter faces and 253,608 gray/white-matter faces, with no
white-matter/CSF faces. The median CSF/gray-matter edge is 1.51~mm
(interquartile range 1.37--1.66). At each interface node, each side's field
is averaged over its incident interface-face element samples; the normal
is the normalized sum of their area vectors. Both sides use the
balanced-envelope-maximizing direction $\mathbf n^*$ of the recovered
gray-matter nodal fields. This direction is not reoptimized for the
side-averaged fields. Fixed-projection ratios retain high-side values
exceeding $10^{-12}$ times that side's map maximum.
}

\DIFadd{For a continuum interface between isotropic conductivities, let
$r=\sigma_{\rm high}/\sigma_{\rm low}\geq1$, where ``high'' and ``low''
identify the more and less conductive sides, and let $\boldsymbol\nu$ be
the unit interface normal. Tangential-field and normal-current continuity give
}\begin{equation}
\DIFadd{\mathbf E_{j,\rm low}=A\mathbf E_{j,\rm high},\qquad
A=\mathbf 1+(r-1)\boldsymbol\nu\boldsymbol\nu^{\mathsf T},\qquad j=1,2,
\label{eq:fem-interface-transform}
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{where $\mathbf 1$ is the identity. The symmetric matrix $A$ has eigenvalues
$1,1,r$. For unit $\mathbf n$, define
$\mathbf m=A\mathbf n/|A\mathbf n|$; then
$\mathbf n\cdot A\mathbf E_j=|A\mathbf n|\,\mathbf m\cdot\mathbf E_j$,
with $1\leq|A\mathbf n|\leq r$.
For the map bounds, the scalar field-to-polarization coupling is the same
on both sides. Let $M_k$ denote a map's maximum over unit orientation, with degree $k=1$
for envelope excursion and $k=2$ for }\DIFaddend quadratic excursion \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$1.04$
at
1:1) but keeps every direction of focus displacement: envelope $2.0$--$3.8$~mm anterior, $\mathcal I$ $3.2$--$5.9$~mm posterior, quadratic excursion zero.
Along the radial direction the displacements also keep their directions (envelope $1.9$--$2.8$~mm
anterior,
$\mathcal I$ $2.0$--$3.7$~mm posterior)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{or mean-square
polarization. For nonzero $M_{k,\rm high}$}\DIFaddend ,
\DIFaddbegin \begin{equation}
\DIFadd{1\leq\frac{M_{k,\rm low}}{M_{k,\rm high}}\leq r^k.
\label{eq:fem-interface-bound}
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{The upper bound follows by maximizing the projection identity. For the lower
bound, choose $\mathbf n$ parallel to $A^{-1}\mathbf m$ for a high-side
maximizer $\mathbf m$; its scale factor is at least one. Field magnitude
obeys the corresponding degree-one bound. Here $r=4.475$ at CSF/gray matter
and $2.667$ at gray/white matter, giving squared bounds 20.03 and 7.11.
}

\DIFadd{A generic fixed oblique projection has neither the floor one nor the ceiling
$r^k$: normal and tangential contributions can cancel on either side.
An exact low-side envelope maximizer nevertheless retains the envelope floor,
and an exact high-side maximizer retains its ceiling. Gray matter is the
low side at CSF/gray matter and the high side at gray/white matter, }\DIFaddend but the
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{ratios change more than with }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{measured $\mathbf n^*$ maximizes recovered nodal fields, not the side averages
used for jumps. Optimization and field averaging must therefore be
distinguished when applying the continuum bounds.
}

\begin{table}[htbp]
\color{blue}
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrrr@{}}
\toprule
Low/high ratio & Nodes & Envelope & Quadratic beat & Mean square\\
\midrule
GM/CSF & 120,892 & 1.17 (1.04--1.53) & 1.28 (1.06--2.07) & 1.25 (1.04--1.97)\\
WM/GM & 126,426 & 1.13 (1.03--1.38) & 1.33 (1.08--2.01) & 1.34 (1.08--2.05)\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{\color{blue}Projected map jumps along the same $\mathbf n^*$ on both sides,
median (interquartile range). GM denotes gray matter and WM white matter.
The conductivity bounds in Eq.~\eqref{eq:fem-interface-bound} apply to
each map's own orientation maximum, not these projections.}
\label{tab:fem-interface-jumps}
\end{table}

\DIFadd{The median $\mathbf n^*$ angle to the interface normal is $64^\circ$ at
CSF/gray matter and $63^\circ$ at gray/white matter. Across maps and interfaces,
4.4--12.8\% of the projected ratios lie below one and 0.2--2.9\% exceed
$r^k$. The largest envelope and quadratic ratios are $7.2\times10^4$ and
$1.29\times10^5$, respectively; large ratios can arise from small denominators
and do not measure large absolute exposure.
}

\DIFadd{Even separately optimized maps depart from the continuum bounds in the
recovered fields (Table~\ref{tab:fem-interface-tails}): 96.7--98.3\% lie
within them, 1.6--3.3\% below one, and fewer than 0.05\% above the ceiling.
First percentiles are 0.988--0.997, but minima reach 0.686.
}

\begin{table}[htbp]
\color{blue}
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrr@{}}
\toprule
Interface & Map & Below 1 & Minimum & Above $r^k$ & Maximum & p1\\
\midrule
CSF/GM & Envelope & 3513 & 0.885 & 33 & 7.90 & 0.995\\

\DIFadd{Component measurements quantify these departures without establishing their
cause. The high-side normal fraction of $\mathbf E_1$ has nodal medians
20\% and 46\% at the two interfaces. Applying the continuum transform to
each high-side field predicts median projected jumps of 1.26--1.80.
For faces whose high-side normal component is at least 10\% of field magnitude,
the realized $\mathbf E_1$ normal jump is 2.15 (1.45--3.18) at CSF/gray matter
and 1.88 (1.51--2.24) at gray/white matter. Varying that cutoff from 0 to 50\%
gives medians 1.97--2.17 and 1.88--1.90; the corresponding $\mathbf E_2$
ranges are 1.99--2.17 and 1.86--1.88. Element-face tangential continuity holds
to rounding (largest residual $5.2\times10^{-11}$ of the high-side field).
Nodal side averages do not retain this identity: their median tangential
residuals are about 1\% of the field.
}

\DIFadd{At nodes above }\DIFaddend the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{label: $1.62$ for the envelope }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{own-maximum ceiling, the median realized $\mathbf E_1$
normal jump exceeds $r$ for all six map/interface combinations; the envelope
subsets give 7.08 }\DIFaddend and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$1.97$ for the quadratic excursion at 1: 1. In
this subject the assumed orientation,
more than the
label, sets the
absolute contrast. Across all 32 combinations of montage, label, ROI tissue definition and focus fraction,
every envelope shift is anterior ($0.7$--$8.1$~mm), every shift of $\mathcal I$ is
posterior ($0.5$--$10.6$~mm)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{2.80. Tangential residuals here are normalized by the
high-side tangential magnitude; their medians occupy the 60th--75th
percentiles of all-node residuals. Below-floor subsets have median normal
ratios 0.81--1.14. These conditional medians associate the map tails with
normal-component scatter; they do not determine the cause of every violation.
Continuous linear finite elements enforce the normal-current condition in
the weak formulation, not face by face. Whether refinement closes the gap
remains unresolved; refining this mesh would also retain its segmented boundary.
}

\DIFadd{``Near'' means within 2~mm of an interface vertex or face centroid.
Gray-matter 99th percentiles near CSF are 0.90}\DIFaddend , \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{0.86, and 0.93 times those
farther away for envelope, quadratic, and mean-square maps. White-matter
ratios near the gray/white interface are 1.25, 1.62, and 1.57. Of each map's
top 1\% of brain nodes, 67--73\% are white matter and 81--82\% lie near the
gray/white interface, compared with 73\% of all brain nodes; 24--31\% lie
near CSF, compared with 47.5\% of all nodes.
}

\DIFadd{A separate check holds the projection along the spatially fixed $x$ direction.
A strict local maximum exceeds every node sharing a tetrahedron edge within

Boundary fractions of mean-square maxima are 98.0\% in gray matter and
96.5\% in white matter, against boundary fractions of 90.0\% and 73.4\%
among all nodes. The quadratic beat gives similar fractions, 97.9\% and
96.1\%, despite lacking the subharmonic restriction. Mean-square interior
maxima number 262 of 13,144 in gray matter and 227 of 6,395 in white matter;
the quadratic counts are 270 of 12,659 }\DIFaddend and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{every shift of the quadratic excursion is zero.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{245 of 6,262. With 95.4\% of all
brain nodes already within 2~mm of an interface, these mesh-dependent
statistics do not sharply test the continuum restriction in \S\ref{sec:dc}.
}\DIFaddend
