\subsection{\DIFdel{Coupling sets the gain at fixed detection}}%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \subsection{\DIFadd{JR gain, mean shifts, and the tACS comparison}}\DIFaddend \label{sec:jcurve}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The resonance sweeps above drove the column toward the Hopf with
the external input
$p$, but $p$ also shifts the operating point, entangling detection ($\Sig''$) with
amplification. To isolate amplification, we vary the JR global connectivity $C$ and solve analytically for the
drive }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{For the AM surrogate, pinning $v^*=8.30$~mV through }\DIFaddend $p(C)$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{that
\emph{pins} the field-free operating point $v^*$, so the detection gain $\Sig''(v^*)$ and the
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{fixes the curvature and }\DIFaddend open-loop
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{detector amplitude $A_{\rm open}\simeq\half|\Sig''(v^*)|\varepsilon^2 m$ are held
constant.
As $C\!\to\!C^*$ (the Hopf, $C^*\!\approx\!137$) the damping
$\gamma\!\to\!0$, the demodulated response grows $\sim30\times$ and the envelope
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{beat $A_{\rm open}\simeq\half|\Sig''(v^*)|\varepsilon^2m$.
As $C\to C^*\approx137$, damping tends to zero, the response grows about
$30$-fold, and }\DIFaddend resonance sharpens (Fig.~\ref{fig:jcurve}a,b)\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{.
This isolates }\DIFaddend a network effect\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{since
the local detector curvature is fixed while the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{: }\DIFaddend recurrent transfer changes \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. Its modal frequency and
input--output projections vary with $C$ as well as its damping. }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{at fixed detector curvature.
}\DIFaddend The rate-to-voltage gain $A_\Omega/A_{\rm open}$ (mV\,s) \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{tracks }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{follows
}\DIFaddend $1/\gamma$ before saturating \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{near criticality
(Fig.~\ref{fig:jcurve}c); driving the same column to the Hopf through the input }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{(panel~c). Approaching the Hopf through }\DIFaddend $p$
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{rather than the coupling $C$ gives a similar leading gain--$\gamma$ trend. Both paths approach a weakly damped mode; their finite-distance gains need not
coincide because
the modal projections can differ}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{gives similar leading scaling. Finite-distance gains can differ because
modal frequencies and input--output projections vary along each path}\DIFaddend .

\begin{figure}[t!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_jcurve.pdf}
\caption{\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\textbf{Coupling controls the amplification at fixed detection (Jansen--Rit).}
The input is the }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{\textbf{JR coupling controls amplification at fixed detection.}
}\DIFaddendFL AM surrogate\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{of }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{, }\DIFaddendFL Eq.~\eqref{eq:amsur}\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{.
The field-free operating point $v^*$ is pinned by co-solving }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{, with }\DIFaddendFL $p(C)$ \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{pinning the field-free state}\DIFaddendFL ,
\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{so }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{curvature }\DIFaddendFL $\Sig''(v^*)=-0.151$~s$^{-1}$mV$^{-2}$\DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{, }\DIFaddendFL and \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{source }\DIFaddendFL $A_{\rm open}$\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{are held constant throughout}\DIFdelendFL .
\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\emph{(a)} }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{\emph{(a,b)} }\DIFaddendFL The \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{demodulated $A_\Omega$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{response }\DIFaddendFL grows \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{and its resonance sharpens }\DIFaddendFL as \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{the
coupling }\DIFdelendFL $C$ approaches
\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{the Hopf }\DIFdelendFL $C^*$ \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{from }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{on }\DIFaddendFL the stable-focus side ($\gamma>0$). \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\emph{(b)} The envelope resonance
sharpens and grows as $C\!\to\!C^*$. }\DIFdelendFL \emph{(c)} \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{The network }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{Rate-to-voltage }\DIFaddendFL gain
$A_\Omega/A_{\rm open}$ \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{, in }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{(}\DIFaddendFL mV\,s\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{, }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{) }\DIFaddendFL follows $1/\gamma$ (dashed) \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{and saturates near the
bifurcation}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{before saturating}\DIFaddendFL .}
\label{fig:jcurve}
\end{figure}

\DIFdelbegin \subsection{\DIFdel{The resonance amplifies the envelope-locked modulation more than the mean rate}}%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Beat modulation and mean rate.}}\DIFaddend \label{sec:timing}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Pinning $v^*$ to hold the local detector
curvature fixed, as in Fig.~\ref{fig:jcurve}, and driving the column toward the Hopf
through the coupling, we read both quantities from the same observable, the pyramidal
rate $r_P=\Sig(v+s)$ with the field in its argument. }\DIFdelend With genuine two tones \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, its
beat-locked modulation }\DIFdelend at $\Delta f=f_0$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{grows $13.2\times$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, the same pinned sweep
increases modulation of $r_P=\Sig(v+s)$ by $13.2$-fold }\DIFaddend ($26$ to $339$~mHz),
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{tracking }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{following }\DIFaddend $1/\gamma$ before \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{saturating; its }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{saturation. The same rate's }\DIFaddend mean shift grows
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$4.4\times$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$4.4$-fold }\DIFaddend ($-1.8$ to $-7.7$~mHz), \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{but stays }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{remaining }\DIFaddend below $0.2\%$ of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{its
}\DIFaddend $3.9$~Hz baseline (Fig.~\ref{fig:timing}). \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The ratio of modulation to mean-shift magnitude }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Their magnitude ratio }\DIFaddend rises
from $14.5$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{far from the Hopf }\DIFdelend to $58$ near $\gamma=1$~s$^{-1}$, then falls to $44$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{closest
to the Hopf as
rectification of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{as
}\DIFaddend the amplified oscillation \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{accelerates the mean shift. The surrogate, whose direct DC source is $3/4$ of the two-tone value, gives ratios
of $19$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{rectifies. In this weakly driven regime, a small
mean change accompanies larger beat modulation}\DIFaddend , \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$66$, and $45$. A small
mean-rate change can therefore accompany a much
larger beat response in this weakly driven regime, }\DIFdelend consistent with primate observations
\cite{vieira2024}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{; both effects are compared in the same }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, with both compared in }\DIFaddend rate units. \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The demodulator feeds the network a constant term and a line at the beat $\Omega$
(Eq.~\eqref{eq:taylor}), and the transfer $G(\Omega)$ of Eq.
~\eqref{eq:gain} treats them
differently}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The surrogate
comparison is supplementary (\S\ref{supp:waveform-checks}).
}

\DIFadd{The detector's steady and beat sources meet different transfers}\DIFaddend :
$|G(\omega_0)|\propto1/(2\gamma\omega_0)$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{diverges as the Hopfis approached}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{grows toward the Hopf}\DIFaddend , while
$|G(0)|\propto1/(\omega_0^2+\gamma^2)$ stays finite. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{This comparison describes
the response to the detector's DC and beat sources. The growing beat
response
itself rectifiesand contributes another mean shift , }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The amplified beat
also rectifies, adding a mean shift }\DIFaddend quadratic in its amplitude \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{.
Before saturation that contribution }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{that }\DIFaddend can
grow as $1/\gamma^2$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, so bounded $G(0)$
does not imply a bounded mean shiftthroughout the approach to the Hopf}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{before saturation. A bounded zero-frequency transfer
therefore does not bound the full mean shift}\DIFaddend .

\DIFdelbegin \subsection{\DIFdel{TI and tACS share the linear amplifier}}%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Comparison with tACS.}}\DIFaddend \label{sec:tacs-results}
TI and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{direct low-frequency stimulation share the same linear amplifier.
Matching }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{tACS share the linear amplifier, so matching }\DIFaddend their generated in-band
drives \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{therefore }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{at a common state }\DIFaddend matches their linear outputs; resonance alone gives
TI no efficiency advantage.
For the AM surrogate with amplitude $\varepsilon_{\rm TI}$ and modulation depth
$m$, and a tACS input of amplitude $\varepsilon_{\rm tACS}$, the ratio of voltage
lock-in amplitudes in linear response is
\[
\mathcal R_{\rm lin}
=\frac{|\Sig''(v^*)|\,\varepsilon_{\rm TI}^2m}{2\Sig'(v^*)\,\varepsilon_{\rm tACS}}.
\]
The common \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{network transfer cancels, so the ratio has no dependence on $\gamma$
when both responses are linear about the same state. At the
amplitudes used in
Figs. ~\ref{fig:jcurve} and ~\ref{fig:tacs}, $\mathcal R_{\rm lin}=0.143$.
The surrogate sweeps give approximately $0.14$ far from the Hopf, rising to $0.24$
at $\gamma=1.07$~s$^{-1}$ and $0.47$ at $0.12$~s$^{-1}$. Genuine two tones give
$0.14$, $0.24$, and $0.46$, respectively, a change of at most $2.2\%$ across the grid.
These are }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{linear transfer cancels. For the stated unequal inputs the predicted ratio is $0.143$;
genuine two-tone responses depart from it near the Hopf. Matching the
leading sources instead gives TI/tACS output ratios of $0.90$--$0.99$
at the three tested couplings. The larger direct input saturates earlier
and shifts the operating state, which field-free pinning does not cancel.
The numerical controls are in the supplement
(\S\ref{supp:tacs-saturation}). These comparisons use unequal inputs;
equal-per-carrier, total-peak, and RMS matching are distinguished in
\S\ref{sec:direct-comparison}.
}

\ifjne\else
\subsection{\DIFadd{TI/tACS ratios and finite-amplitude saturation}}\label{supp:tacs-saturation}
\DIFadd{Table~\ref{tab:waveform-checks} gives the AM and genuine two-tone }\DIFaddend response
ratios for the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{stated unequal input amplitudes , not an
equal-power comparison.In controls at three couplings, matching the tACS input
to TI's leading beat source gives TI/tACS output ratios of $0.99$, $0.94$, and$0.92$ for the
surrogate, and $0.99$, $0.93$, and $0.90$ for two tones. Increasing that direct input by a factor of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{three coupling controls specified in
\S\ref{app:jr-sweep-settings}. The predicted linear ratio is $0.143$ for
the unequal amplitudes in Figs.~\ref{fig:jcurve} and~\ref{fig:tacs}; the
common transfer cancels at a fixed state. The stronger direct response
saturates earlier near the Hopf: multiplying its input by }\DIFaddend $6.99$ increases
its \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{response
by }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{output by only }\DIFaddend $6.97$, $3.95$, and $1.96$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, respectively.
The larger response saturates earlier, with stimulation-induced mean shiftsthat }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{at those three couplings.
Stimulation-induced mean shifts, which }\DIFaddend field-free pinning does not cancel\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{; this accounts for the finite-amplitude
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{,
account for the }\DIFaddend departure from the linear ratio\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{near criticality. }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. These are not equal-RMS
comparisons; matching conventions are given in \S\ref{sec:direct-comparison}.
}\DIFaddend

\DIFaddbegin \fi

\DIFaddend \subsection{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Exact mean-field confirmation: the NMM2 }\DIFdelend PING \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{gamma generator}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{amplifies the dynamic QIF beat source}\DIFaddend }\label{sec:nmm2-results}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The exact PING mean field generates and amplifies the beat through its derived
$v_E^2$ nonlinearity. With a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{With genuine }\DIFaddend $300$~Hz \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{two-tone field entering the excitatory
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{tones entering E }\DIFaddend current, the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{lock-in of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{PING rate }\DIFaddend $r_E$
shows \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{a growing }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{forced }\DIFaddend gamma resonance below the Hopf and a \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{beat }\DIFaddend response superposed
on autonomous gamma above it (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Figs}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Fig}\DIFaddend .~\ref{fig:nmm2_res}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{,~\ref{fig:nmm2_map}}\DIFdelend ).
The resonance \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{ridge intensifies
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{grows }\DIFaddend through the Hopf \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{; its appreciable frequency shift with operating point makes
it diagonal
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and shifts with state, forming a diagonal
ridge in the $(\Delta f,\bar\eta)$ plane of Fig.~\ref{fig:nmm2_map}}\DIFaddend , unlike
the JR \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{sweep shown here }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{example }\DIFaddend \cite{clusella2023}.

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{This derived detector lies on the fast side of its crossover at }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{At }\DIFaddend $300$~Hz\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{:
carrier }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, }\DIFaddend voltage excursions mix through $v_E^2$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{before the rate reads the result.
For the full eight-state PING (Eq.~\eqref{eq:nmm2-ping}), each high-frequency current tone
of amplitude $I_j$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{on the fast side of
the detector's crossover (Fig.~\ref{fig:crossover}a). Each current tone
}\DIFaddend produces a voltage phasor $I_j/(i\omega_j\tau_E)$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{.
The $v_E^2$ term therefore supplies a difference-frequency source
of magnitude
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, giving a beat source
}\DIFaddend $I_1I_2/(\tau_E^2\omega_1\omega_2)$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{inside }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{in }\DIFaddend the equation for $\tau_E\dot v_E$.
After division by $\tau_E$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, this source is propagated }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and propagation }\DIFaddend through the network \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{susceptibility }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{transfer }\DIFaddend at
$\Omega$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. At four stable operating points, the resulting
response agrees with the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, it matches the }\DIFaddend full second-order \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{calculation to }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{response at four stable operating
points }\DIFaddend within $1.1\%$ at $300$~Hz, $0.1\%$ at $1$~kHz, and $0.03\%$
at $2$~kHz (Fig.~\ref{fig:crossover}b).

\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{figure}[t!]
%DIFDELCMD < \centering
%DIFDELCMD < \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_mpr_crossover.pdf}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%
{%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelFL{\textbf{Static curvature at slow carriers, slope at fast ones.} \emph{(a)}
Second-order DC rate shift of an uncoupled MPR population ($\Delta=1$, dimensionless membrane-time variables) under a
current carrier, Eq.~\eqref{eq:crossover}, for operating points below, at and above the
inflection of $F(Q)$. Dotted: the static limit $\tfrac14F''$; dashed: the fast-carrier
limit $F'/(2\tilde\omega^2)$; circles: RK4 integrations at $I_{\rm ac}=0.05$ averaged over whole carrier
periods (relative error below $10^{-3}$). Above the inflection the sign changes at
$\tilde\omega_z$. The horizontal coordinate is $\tilde\omega=\omega\tau_m$; the carrier markers
use $\tau_m=\tau_E=15$~ms. \emph{(b)} PING of Eq.~\eqref{eq:nmm2-ping}: relative error of the
fast-carrier difference-frequency source against the exact second-order response at four
stable operating points ($\gamma$ the damping of the gamma mode), two tones centered at
$300$, $1000$ and $2000$~Hz.}}
%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{fig:crossover}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{figure}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The unpinned PING coupling sweep also approaches a gamma Hopf, but two
modes compete for the response peak. Near the Hopf, the critical mode's
linear gain has a fitted damping exponent of $1.02$, consistent with the
$1/\gamma$ law; the full-sweep exponent is $0.71$. At finite amplitude the response departs from the linear
reference and saturates, while the driven endpoint remains attracting.
The mode tracking, amplitude checks, and their numerical ranges accompany
the coupling curve in the supplement (\S\ref{supp:ping-coupling-results}).
Thus JR and PING share the critical mode's $1/\gamma$ gain law despite
their different detectors; the law does not describe every mode or the
entire finite-amplitude sweep.
}\DIFaddend

Lock-in response of the excitatory rate $r_E$ at the envelope frequency $\Delta f$ under a

\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \ifjne\else
\subsection{\DIFadd{Mode competition and finite-amplitude gain in PING}}\label{supp:ping-coupling-results}
\DIFaddend In NMM2 PING, $J$ is the mean inter-neuron QIF synaptic weight.
Sweeping $J$ at fixed background drive toward the gamma Hopf reveals two
competing modes (Fig.~\ref{fig:nmm2_jcurve}). A second mode near $55$~Hz carries
the linear-response peak over $14.6\lesssim J\lesssim16.2$; the critical mode
carries it at lower couplings and again near the Hopf, where its frequency is
about $50$~Hz. In that near-Hopf interval,
$0.0014\lesssim\gamma\lesssim0.0081$~ms$^{-1}$, the linear gain scales as
$1/\gamma$ with a fitted exponent of $1.02$ over a $5.8$-fold damping range.
The full-sweep exponent is $0.71$, so a single-mode law does not describe the
whole curve. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{This refines the coupling-controlled sensitivity expected in QIF
networks \cite{clusella2023} : both coupling and rectifier are derived, but the
dominant mode can change}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Coupling-dependent sensitivity \cite{clusella2023} therefore
includes changes in which mode carries the peak}\DIFaddend .

At $A_f=8$, the simulated response exceeds the normalized linear reference by
up to $48\%$ in the amplitude check, then saturates. At a quarter of that
amplitude ($A_f=2$, one sixteenth of the power), the departures stay within
$11\%$ at the nine tested couplings. The \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{normalization and endpoint-stability checks are given in
\S\ref{sec:nmm2-methods};
$\gamma$ always describes the field-free critical mode, not the driven state}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{driven endpoint remains attracting under both waveforms
(\S\ref{app:ping-stability})}\DIFaddend .
The alpha JR and gamma NMM2 circuits share the $1/\gamma$ gain scaling of their critical mode despite their different
mixers, with amplification adjustable through coupling.

\DIFaddbegin \fi

envelope in arbitrary units. The last $200$~ms are shown.

near $52$~Hz dominates. \emph{(c,d)} Field off/on at $\bar\eta=1.5$,
beat $42$~Hz: autonomous gamma remains dominant.
Right-hand panels fold rate on beat phase over $44$ and $34$ cycles, respectively,
normalized to each condition's mean (off gray, on blue; scale 0--2).
Folding suppresses non-beat-locked gamma and exposes the smaller beat component;
it does not establish gamma-amplitude gating or phase capture.}}

component. The }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{at the lower tested
amplitude and field-induced gamma at the higher amplitude. Its }\DIFaddend $8000$-neuron populations test

\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Access sets the local polarization, detection generates the beat, and the circuit
sets its amplification. Esmaeilpour et al.\
\cite{esmaeilpour2021} already related network TI sensitivity to short, axon-like
membrane times and distinguished sensitivity from selectivity mediated by
network
adaptation. Here the exact QIF mean field connects static and dynamic
mixing, the weak-input product law determines spatial weighting, and matching
the generated drive exposes the linear amplifier shared by TI and tACS}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \subsection{\DIFadd{Cellular access and mixing require separate calibration}}
\DIFadd{The JR and QIF models demonstrate local mixing followed by recurrent
amplification. Cellular mixing \cite{mirzakhalili2020,luff2024mixer} and network
amplification \cite{caldas2024,karimi2024,esmaeilpour2021} have complementary
experimental and modeling support, but their combined transfer at physiological
kHz fields remains unmeasured. The short-time cases in Table~\ref{tab:doors}
are access assumptions; varying the effective membrane time quantifies their
consequences}\DIFaddend .

At \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{extracellular }\DIFdelend fields of order $1$~V/m, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{voltage-gated }\DIFdelend channels respond through membrane polarization\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{: the direct protein effects reviewed by Cifra et al.\
\cite{cifra2026} occur at $10$~kV/m to GV/m,
with protocol- and molecule-dependent
thresholds; that review excludes fields below $0.1$~kV/m, where it expects direct effects to be negligible against thermal noise.Presynaptic terminals }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{,
well below fields associated with direct protein effects
\cite{cifra2026}.}\footnote{\DIFadd{That review describes direct effects at $10$~kV/m
to GV/m, depending on molecule and protocol; it excludes fields below
$0.1$~kV/m, where it expects direct effects to be negligible against thermal noise.}}
\DIFadd{Terminals }\DIFaddend can polarize more strongly than somata
\cite{rahman2013,aberra2023,chakraborty2018}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, but small
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. Small }\DIFaddend size alone does not imply
fast charging: specific membrane resistance times capacitance is area-independent\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, while }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{;
}\DIFaddend conductance, geometry, and stimulation mode determine \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the response. }%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Table~\ref{tab:doors} summarizes the measurements and models that motivate shorter
access times ; their transfer under TI remains to be calibrated}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{access. The supplementary
comparison distinguishes these mechanisms from strong-pulse charging
(\S\ref{supp:access}).
}\ifjne\else
\subsection{\DIFadd{Access assumptions in related models}}
\DIFadd{Short access times motivate the model but remain preparation-dependent}\DIFaddend .
The $1$ and $0.4$~ms \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{network sensitivity }\DIFdelend cases of \cite{esmaeilpour2021} precede \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the present }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{our }\DIFaddend effective-time
hypothesis. Luff et al.'s pulse-width-modulated TI \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(PWM-TI) model uses a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{model uses }\DIFaddend $1$~ms \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{passive response }\DIFdelend within a
$1$--$100$~ms \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{parameter }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{passive-time }\DIFaddend sweep \cite{luff2024}. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Separately, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The $0.7~\mu$s charging
measured by }\DIFaddend Semenov et al.\ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\cite{semenov2025} measured field-induced membrane charging near $0.7~\mu$s in
isolated neurons under strong , }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{under strong }\DIFaddend microsecond field pulses
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. This is }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\cite{semenov2025} concerns }\DIFaddend a different response mode from intracellular
current charging or periaxonal conduction. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Access time and field coupling remain preparation-dependent. }\DIFdelend A $10$--$100$-fold reduction in
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{a }\DIFdelend QIF membrane time is therefore a sensitivity hypothesis\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. Its
population-level consequence can be studied through the existing }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, whose population
consequence follows from the }\DIFaddend slow-synapse limit\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, with each physical filter included once; a new periaxonal circuit is not
needed for that limiting argument}\DIFdelend .

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{At weak input, smooth cellular contributions with a nonzero second-order term
combine into a quadratic mixing coefficient.QIF derives one realization without
requiring identification of each channel. It connects cellular mixing
\cite{mirzakhalili2020,luff2024mixer} with the network dependence seen in slices
and simulations \cite{caldas2024,karimi2024}. Cellular mixing and circuit amplification are therefore not competing explanations.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \fi
\DIFaddend

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Weak amplitude alone does not make that coefficient static. Fast population
relaxation relative to synapses gives the NMM2 slow-synapse limit; relaxation
must also be fast relative to the carrier for curvature to describe its mixing. Outside that limit the
coefficient is dynamic. Its magnitude,
phase, and frequency dependence remain model-specific, while the static slope converts
slow input into rate and enters recurrent gain (\S\ref{sec:nmm})}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Missey et al.\ \cite{missey2026} report biomarker suppression with both
low- and high-kHz carriers. Their Methods specify $1300/1430$~Hz at one
center and $9000/9130$~Hz at the other centers. For these pairs, the
$0.2$~ms point-membrane model predicts a high/low source ratio of $0.030$
at matched local fields. This comparison challenges that access hypothesis
but does not test it directly: carrier band was confounded with center,
local polarization was unmeasured, and a nonlinear or saturating physiological
readout could reconcile different sources with similar biomarker changes}\DIFaddend .

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{A spatial TI dose map depends on this physiology.
The usual envelope-modulation
map assumes a response determined by envelope excursion; the square-law map instead weights it by the larger local polarization (Eq. ~\eqref{eq:reweight}).
Its accompanying DC source follows the summed carrier intensities under the
conditions of \S\ref{sec:dc}, providing an operating-point shift that
the equal-RMS carrier control isolates and that can cross the population Hopf
(\S\ref{sec:qif}).
Both idealized mean maps obey the boundary-maximum restriction of
\S\ref{sec:dc};
the beat map does not .
Orientation, access, operating point, and network susceptibility further shape
the response. }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \subsection{\DIFadd{Detector laws predict different spatial responses}}
\DIFadd{The head-model comparison separates geometric consequences of detector laws.
The product and summed-squares maps differ from the envelope map even before
state-dependent detection and network gain are applied. The fixed quadratic
pattern follows algebraically from fixed field shapes and linear access;
opposite envelope and mean-square centroid motion is a result for the tested
subject and montages, not a general steering law. }\DIFaddend A sharp rectifier can \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{approximate
envelope following }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{follow
the envelope }\DIFaddend over a finite amplitude range \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{; }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{while }\DIFaddend smoothing or heterogeneity
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{can restore }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{restores }\DIFaddend quadratic behavior at smaller inputs. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The amplitude and harmonic tests in \S\ref{sec:transduction-methods} distinguish these regimes}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Amplitude and harmonic sweeps
therefore complement the spatial comparison}\DIFaddend .

\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The connection to }\DIFaddend Violante et al.\ \cite{violante2023} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{demonstrated envelope steering and a change in hippocampal physiology using different measurements. Their field
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{is a hypothesis generated
by these maps. Their }\DIFaddend models and cadaver recordings supported anterior envelope
steering \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{when the
current ratio changed }\DIFdelend from $2+2$~mA \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(1:1) }\DIFdelend to $1+3$~mA\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(1:3) through the same
electrode pairs. In participants, stimulation was confined to the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. During participants' }\DIFaddend 32~s encoding \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{stage of each block. The }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{blocks,
}\DIFaddend 1:3 \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{condition reduced the encoding-evoked
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{reduced the evoked }\DIFaddend blood-oxygen-level-dependent (BOLD) response relative
to sham ($P=0.006$) and 1:1 ($P=0.015$); 1:1 did not differ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{significantly }\DIFdelend from sham
($P=0.953$). The \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{reduction was nominally largest anteriorly, but the }\DIFdelend stimulation-by-region interaction was \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{not significant }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{nonsignificant
}\DIFaddend ($P=0.76$)\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. The voxelwise sham--1:3 comparison
found significant reductions in }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, so the physiology did not establish spatial steering. Significant
voxelwise reductions extended beyond the anterior focus: }\DIFaddend 30\% of anterior,
25\% of posterior, and 2\% of middle hippocampal voxels.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Significant BOLD reductions thus extended beyond
the anterior envelope focus.
}\DIFdelend

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{All three geometric maps change between these current conditions, so a condition
difference alone cannot identify the detector. In our }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{In }\DIFaddend IXI002\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{calculation
(\S\ref{sec:fem-results}, Fig.~\ref{fig:fem}), the envelope focus moves 2.5~mm
anteriorly, whereas the quadratic beat retains its pattern and falls to $3/4$
of its value everywhere at fixed neural state. The }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, }\DIFaddend mean-square polarization \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{changes by $\mathcal I_{1:3}/\mathcal I_{1:1}
=(\mathcal P_1^2+9\mathcal P_2^2)/[4(\mathcal P_1^2+\mathcal P_2^2)]$,
exceeding one only where $|\mathcal P_2|>\sqrt{3/5}|\mathcal P_1|
\approx0.775|\mathcal P_1|$. Its hippocampal mean }\DIFdelend rises by 24\% \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{and its focus
moves 4.5~mm posteriorly. Equal summed currents therefore do not match local
RMS polarization.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The mean-square map also predicts a physiological effect:at second order it
sets the steady rate source $Y_{\rm DC}=\tfrac12\Sig''(v^*)\mathcal I$
(\S\ref{sec:dc}). With spatially uniform, fixed curvature, its 24\% increase
in hippocampal mean
predicts a 24\% larger mean-rate source in magnitude,
with the sign set by the curvature. Recurrence then determines the final rate
response. By comparison, the hippocampal mean }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{under 1:3, while mean
}\DIFaddend envelope excursion falls by 45\% \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, and both the projected and isotropic }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and both }\DIFaddend quadratic beat maps fall by 25\%.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{A response linear in the quadratic beat thus decreases; its own rectified
mean shift }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{At fixed uniform static curvature the steady-source magnitude therefore rises
by 24\%; a mean shift from rectification of the beat instead }\DIFaddend scales as
$(3/4)^2\approx0.56$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{at fixed state }\DIFdelend in the weak-response limit\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(\S\ref{sec:timing}). Of these hippocampal map averages, only the
mean-square polarization increases under 1:3, consistent with a larger
magnitude of BOLD reduction }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. The first ordering is
consistent with stronger BOLD suppression }\DIFaddend if the steady source lowers activity
and the hemodynamic response preserves that ordering. A slow \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, approximately linear hemodynamic readout
suppresses the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{linear readout
suppresses a }\DIFaddend zero-mean $5$~Hz beat \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{at first order }\DIFdelend while retaining a \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{slow }\DIFdelend shift over the stimulation
window\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. Such a shift can add to
the encoding-evoked response or alter its operating point}\DIFdelend ; nonlinear neural or vascular responses can also convert \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{that }\DIFaddend beat into
a mean change \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{or alter the encoding response}\DIFaddend .

This \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{interpretation remains conditional:
the envelope excursion rises where $|\mathcal P_1|>2|\mathcal P_2|$, reaching $1.5\times$ where $|\mathcal P_1|\geq3|\mathcal P_2|$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{comparison does not identify the biological detector. Local envelope excursion can also increase under 1:3
(supplement, \S\ref{supp:steering-algebra})}\DIFaddend , allowing a \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{spatially }\DIFdelend selective or
thresholded envelope response\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. Equal summed currents do not match local RMS polarization}\DIFaddend ,
and the nonsignificant 1:1--sham \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{difference
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{result }\DIFaddend does not establish a null effect.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Neither hippocampal curvature nor the
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Heterogeneous curvature and unknown }\DIFaddend recurrent and hemodynamic gains \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{are calibrated here; spatially varying
curvature can also change
the mean-source ratio. Neither the BOLD contrast
nor the reported functional-connectivity changes directly measure the beat or the steady source.
The voxelwise pattern therefore cannot be matched to
one of these maps without a neural and hemodynamic readout model. A spatially resolved beat measurement at controlled state would
test envelope
steering against the fixed quadratic pattern; an equal-power
unmodulated-carrier control would isolate effects of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{can change
}\DIFaddend the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{steady source under
the
second-order matching conditions of \S\ref{sec:dc}.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{predicted ordering. Measuring spatially resolved steady and beat responses
at controlled polarization and state, with an equal-RMS carrier control,
would discriminate the hypotheses.
}\ifjne\else

\DIFadd{The mean-square polarization
changes by $\mathcal I_{1:3}/\mathcal I_{1:1}
=(\mathcal P_1^2+9\mathcal P_2^2)/[4(\mathcal P_1^2+\mathcal P_2^2)]$,
exceeding one only where $|\mathcal P_2|>\sqrt{3/5}|\mathcal P_1|

\subsection{\DIFadd{Field-induced oscillations and resonant amplification}}
\DIFadd{At stronger fields, }\DIFaddend TI and unmodulated \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{kHz fields }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{carriers }\DIFaddend can have similar excitation
thresholds \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, with modulation patterning firing rather than lowering the threshold
\cite{opancar2025,semenov2025}; some peripheral responses do not
require envelope extraction \cite{peripheral2023}. The }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\cite{opancar2025,semenov2025}, and peripheral responses need not
extract the envelope \cite{peripheral2023}. Our }\DIFaddend weak-input calculation \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{instead describes
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{concerns
}\DIFaddend modulation of ongoing activity \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\cite{vieira2024,missey2026}, excluding direct
carrier-evoked spiking and conduction block. The }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\cite{vieira2024}; the }\DIFaddend finite-amplitude
QIF examples \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{show a related circuit transition: the mean shift crosses the Hopf, inducing
gamma alongside the beat response. Its }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{instead show a mean shift crossing a circuit Hopf. That }\DIFaddend boundary
depends on \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{both field amplitude and the initial distance from the bifurcation. These examples and the laminar extension go beyond the weak-input expansion without establishing physiological
thresholds}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{amplitude and initial state, and supplies no physiological threshold.
The supplementary laminar extension tests two resonant bands at low carriers
(\S\ref{app:lanmm}), outside TI's kHz regime}\DIFaddend .

The \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{recurrent amplifier is shared with direct low-frequency stimulation. Our
coupling sweep fixes the JR field-free operating point and its local curvature
while changing recurrent gain. A direct in-band drive shows the same
$1/\gamma$ growth and resonance sharpening (Fig.~\ref{fig:tacs})}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{common TI/tACS amplifier follows from matching source, readout, and state}\DIFaddend ,
consistent with \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the known resonant susceptibility of recurrent }\DIFdelend excitatory--inhibitory \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{networks
\cite{ledoux2011,clusella2023}
. Near the Hopf,
this is temporal critical slowing in a mean-field model, not evidence of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{models \cite{ledoux2011,clusella2023}
and related tACS/AM phase-locking studies
\cite{ali2013,negahbani2018,krause2023}. Stimulation-induced state shifts can
change the finite-amplitude ratio. These models describe a single column,
so their near-Hopf temporal slowing does not establish }\DIFaddend a diverging spatial
correlation length. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Related models already reproduce tACS entrainment and AM-driven phase locking
\cite{ali2013,negahbani2018,krause2023}. When TI and tACS are matched
for effective in-band input, observable, and operating state, they share the linear amplifier.
Within linear response, resonance alone therefore gives
TI
no efficiency advantage. At finite amplitude the response ratio can change near
the Hopf, as the coupling sweep demonstrates (\S\ref{sec:tacs-results}); its value then
depends on drive amplitude and the driven state as well as on field-free damping.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{With reverberation time
$\tau_{\rm rev}=1/\gamma$, the quality factor is $\mathcal Q=\pi f_0\tau_{\rm rev}$
and power bandwidth approximately $1/(\pi\tau_{\rm rev})$.
Relaxation over a few hundred milliseconds \cite{wilting2018} gives
order-ten quality and order-$1$~Hz bandwidth at $10$~Hz, making detuning
consequential; that measured relaxation is not specific to an alpha mode.
}\DIFaddend

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Absolute response estimates remain sensitive to access and transduction.
Measured DC field-to-voltage }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \subsection{\DIFadd{Efficiency depends on the generated drive}}
\DIFadd{Measured DC }\DIFaddend couplings are $0.12$--$0.18$~mV per V/m in hippocampus
($0.21$ at $10$~Hz\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{AC}\DIFdelend ) \cite{bikson2004,deans2007} and reach \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{about
}\DIFdelend $0.5$~mV per V/m
in cortical L5/6 pyramidal cells \cite{radman2009}; human cortical fields near
$0.8$~V/m have been reported at $2$~mA \cite{huang2017,huang2017corr}.
These \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{static couplings do
not fix the coupling at kHz }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{measurements constrain illustrative input budgets, not the kHz transfer.
The $2$--$5\%$ source comparison in \S\ref{sec:direct-comparison} matches
each TI carrier's polarization to the direct input}\DIFaddend . \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{For equal TI carrierpolarizations $\varepsilon$,
the JR beat drive is $\Sig''\varepsilon^2/2$, whereas a direct drive of the same
peak polarization gives $2\Sig'\varepsilon$. Their leading amplitude ratio is
$\mathcal R_{\rm det}\simeq|\Sig''|\varepsilon/(4\Sig')$: the relative efficiency decreases
as the inputis weakened in this perturbative regime. A larger recurrent gain
amplifies both drives.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Matching the total TI
peak instead halves the ratio to
$\mathcal R_{\rm det}\simeq|\Sig''|\varepsilon/(4\Sig')$; common recurrent
gain cancels in linear response.
}\DIFaddend

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{For scale, a reverberation time $\tau_{\rm rev}=1/\gamma$ gives a near-Hopf
quality factor $\mathcal Q=\pi f_0\tau_{\rm rev}$ and power bandwidth
approximately $1/(\pi\tau_{\rm rev})$. Population relaxation over a few hundred
milliseconds \cite{wilting2018} would give a quality factor of order ten at
$10$~Hz and a bandwidth of order $1$~Hz. This is an order-of-magnitude comparison:
the measured relaxation is not specific to the alpha mode. Narrow amplification
also makes detuning consequential.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Primate measurements illustrate the limits of an efficiency comparison.
Vieira et al.\ \cite{vieira2024} reported two distinct experiments. Their
two-pair TI
comparison gave a phase-locking effect ratio of $0.34$ against
tACS (95\% confidence interval $0.04$--$0.65$, 65 of 154 neurons), without
correcting for TI currents up to
$\pm2.5$~mA per pair versus $\pm1$~mA for tACS.
A separate single-pair AM-tACS control compensated for frequency-dependent
shunting to match extracellular modulation depth. Of twenty cells, ten responded
to either modality and seven to both; in the latter group, median phase-locking
value (PLV) was $0.10$ for AM-tACS and $0.20$ for tACS, against $0.043$ at baseline.
The reported TI modulation depths were about $0.5$--$0.62$~V/m. These weaker
TI and AM effects are compatible with a weaker generated beat drive, but neither
experiment matched polarization at the mixer, and PLV depends on baseline and
state. They therefore do not calibrate this paper's detector coefficient; that
requires the same timing readout across a local-polarization amplitude sweep.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend For \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{an illustrative fast-access budget, take a per-tone field }\DIFdelend $E_j=0.5$~V/m, $\lambda_0=0.5$~mV per V/m, $f_c=2$~kHz \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, }\DIFdelend and the assumed
$\tau_m=0.2$~ms\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{.
Then $\varepsilon=\lambda_0E_j|H_m|\approx0.092$~mV, rounded }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, access gives $\varepsilon\approx0.092$~mV. Rounding }\DIFaddend to
$0.1$~mV \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{for
scale; this }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{yields $\mathcal R_{\rm det}\approx0.8\%$ at $v^*=8.30$~mV
under total-peak matching. This }\DIFaddend is an assumed \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{coupling }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{input }\DIFaddend budget, not an inferred
polarization in \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the }\DIFdelend recorded cells.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{At $v^*=8.30$~mV the JR detector gives
$\mathcal R_{\rm det}\approx0.8\%$ for $0.1$~mV, for the generated drive relative to a matched-peak direct input.
The smooth JR detector is therefore weak under this access budget, even though
recurrence can amplify its output. A measured PLV effect cannot be compared
directly with this drive ratio; a polarization-calibrated amplitude sweep with
the
same readout would test the estimate.
}\DIFdelend

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{For comparison}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{For balanced TI and a direct sinusoid matched in peak polarization at the
same operating point}\DIFaddend , the carrier-averaged \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, matched-peak, }\DIFdelend open-loop \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{drive }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{fundamental-amplitude
}\DIFaddend ratio has a numerically estimated supremum of $0.348$ over bounded\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{,
}\DIFaddend nondecreasing memoryless transfers (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{App.~\ref{app:bound}). The positive-mixture-of-steps argument extends
the logistic result to this class. Approaching it requires an active transition
sharp relative to the input; for the logistic family at the illustrative
polarizations , this means microvolt widths. The limiting detector is step-like
and has a second-harmonic ratio near $0.15$, distinct from both weak
square-law detection and linear envelope following. This bound does not cover
the dynamic QIF detector or all cellular mechanisms. A larger common matched
polarization does not exceed the class bound; unequal actual polarizations can
instead invalidate the matching.
The unequal-current TI ratio and the matched-depth
AM-tACS control do not measure this matched-peak, open-loop ratio.
Thus approaching the ceiling would require leaving the smooth weak-input regime
used here; within that regime, local curvature determines the generated drive.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{supplement, \S\ref{app:bound},
Eq.~\eqref{eq:etasup}). Dynamic QIF is outside this detector class.
}\DIFaddend

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Access and mixing require calibration; their values are not fixed by the
quadratic law, and cascading ordinary synaptic filters cannot improve access
because they attenuate the carrier before a subsequent detector receives it. The response also depends on the neuron model}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Vieira et al.'s weaker TI effects \cite{vieira2024} are compatible with a
smaller beat source, but their local mixer polarizations were unmatched
(supplement, \S\ref{supp:vieira}). Phase-locking value (PLV) also depends on
baseline and state. A calibrated amplitude sweep with a common readout is
needed to estimate the detector coefficient.
}

\subsection{\DIFadd{Limits of the population description}}
\DIFadd{Synaptic filters before a detector further attenuate its input. Model choice
also matters}\DIFaddend : Hodgkin--Huxley and Frankenhaeuser--Huxley models reproduce
stimulus-evoked firing missed by the tested leaky, exponential, and
adaptive-exponential integrate-and-fire models \cite{plovie2025}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, without invalidating the weak-input expansion}\DIFdelend .
Noise, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{heterogeneity beyond the assumed excitability distribution}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{additional heterogeneity}\DIFaddend , and intercolumn interactions can broaden or
shift \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{our }\DIFaddend deterministic resonances. Human \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{evidence
is mixed, including }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{findings include }\DIFaddend peripheral effects
without central effects \cite{iszak2023} and null occipito-parietal alpha-power
modulation \cite{mansourinezhad2025}. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Testing this mechanism therefore requires access, generated beat, and network
tuning measured }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Access, generated sources, and circuit
tuning therefore need measurement }\DIFaddend in the same preparation.

\subsection{Falsifiable predictions and tests}\label{sec:predictions}
Table~\ref{tab:predictions} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{separates tests of access, detection, and amplification
from observations already consistent with the model. Quantitative reanalysis of
the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{distinguishes the measurements needed to test each
stage. Interpreting }\DIFaddend timing and synaptic-blockade data requires \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the amplitude and harmonic controls
of \S\ref{sec:transduction-methods}, with detector and network changes measured
separately}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{independent
measurements of amplitude scaling, harmonic content, and recurrent gain}\DIFaddend .

\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begingroup%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{At resonance with $\mathcal Q=10$, a detector ratio of $20\%$ appears as only $0.7\%$,
which could make an envelope follower look quadratic. Driving off resonance
at $\Delta f\simeq f_0/4$ places both harmonics on the flatter part of the
response, where $|G(2\Omega)/G(\Omega)|\approx1.22$--$1.25$ for
$\mathcal Q=3$--$30$. Alternatively, a weak direct tACS sweep can measure
$G$ independently so its effect can be removed from the harmonic ratio.
}

hippocampal oscillations \cite{esmaeilpour2020}.
}%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{Measure each local polarization while sweeping $f_c$ at fixed beat and state}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{Sweep carrier frequency and tonic state at fixed
beat. Measure local polarization and network gain separately}\DIFaddendFL ; normalize the
\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{weak beat by their product . Hold network gain fixed or measure it
independently to separate access from residual detector dependence. Measure any
residual cutoff rather than assigning it to a compartment. }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{beat source by the carrier product and compare its magnitude and phase. }\DIFaddendFL \\[4pt]
\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\textbf{Independent carrier amplitudes.} At fixed detector and network gains,
quadratic modulation excursion follows $2|\varepsilon_1\varepsilon_2|$; an ideal envelope
follower tracks $2\min(|\varepsilon_1|,|\varepsilon_2|)$
(Eq.~\eqref{eq:reweight}, Table~\ref{tab:transduction-maps}).
}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{Quadratic versus envelope detection }\DIFaddendFL & \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{The unequal-amplitude spatial example separates these maps; a biological test
with independent local-polarization calibration is not supplied here.
}%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{Hold the weaker polarization fixed and vary the stronger, then swap tones. Keep both inputs weak, frequencies fixed, and the operating state controlled.
Reconstruct the slow-response excursion after correcting network filtering;
a beat lock-in alone does not measure the envelope's full excursion}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{Vary carrier amplitudes independently,
then together. A weak quadratic source follows their product; an envelope
follower follows the minimum. Measure amplitude scaling and harmonics off
resonance or correct their network gains}\DIFaddendFL . \\[4pt]
\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\textbf{Current-ratio steering.} With fixed electrode pairs, frequencies,
access, and neural state, the quadratic beat map scales with
$I_1^{\rm pair}I_2^{\rm pair}$; its normalized pattern does not move.
An envelope follower can instead shift its maximum.
}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{Fixed quadratic spatial pattern }\DIFaddendFL & \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{Cassar\`a et al.\ identify the product-law restriction
\cite{cassara2025part2}. The IXI002 comparison (\S\ref{sec:fem-results}) confirms
the geometric distinction: envelope and mean-square foci move oppositely;
the quadratic beat map only rescales. A physiological beat-map test remains open.
}%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelendFL Vary the current ratio at fixed electrodes\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{and compare normalized beat maps
and target-to-reference ratios, using }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{,
frequencies, and state. Compare normalized physiological beat maps with fixed
local orientations. Use }\DIFaddendFL a cortex-only reference \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{to test deep-target
selectivity. Keep the projection direction fixed at each site,
including any direction chosen to maximize the envelope at a reference ratio.
Measure the DC shift }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{for deep selectivity; measure
steady shifts }\DIFaddendFL and repeat at lower amplitude to \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{check state dependence.
Changing from $2+2$ to $1+3$~mA multiplies envelope excursion by
$0.5$--$1.5$ depending on the local polarization ratio, and the quadratic beat
by $0.75$ everywhere. The mean-square factor is $1.25$ at equal per-current magnitudes,
tending to $0.25$ or $2.25$ where the first or second carrier dominates,
respectively. This last factor weights the signed DC source; it does not
specify excitation or suppression. }%DIFDELCMD < \\[4pt]
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelFL{\textbf{State and carrier dependence.} The static sigmoid follows $\Sig''$ and
reverses sign at its inflection (PEIX$=0$). Fast-carrier QIF mixes in voltage and
reads the source through rate and network susceptibility; its DC term follows
$F'$ without the same sign rule (\S\ref{sec:dynamic}).
}%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{Cell-type-specific responses (PV versus pyramidal) are reported
\cite{caldas2024}; the detector coefficient remains model-dependent}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{test state changes}\DIFaddendFL . \DIFdelbeginFL %DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{Vary tonic drive and carrier frequency at fixed beat; measure beat amplitude
and phase. Compare with the predicted coefficient after accounting for network
susceptibility. Amplitude changesalone do not isolate detection. }\DIFdelendFL \\[4pt]
\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\textbf{Transduction class.} At a matched site, weak smooth detection with nonzero
curvature gives $Y_\Omega\propto\varepsilon^2$ and a harmonic ratio vanishing as
$\varepsilon\to0$. An ideal envelope follower gives $Y_\Omega\propto\varepsilon$
and ratio $1/5$; a threshold has onset $2\varepsilon=\delta$ and a variable ratio.
}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{Resonant amplification }\DIFaddendFL & \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{Untested with local-polarization calibration; existing comparisons match
extracellular field rather than $\varepsilon$.
}%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{Sweep amplitude off resonance, reading both the log--log slope and
$|Y_{2\Omega}/Y_\Omega|$. Near resonance, suppression by approximately
$3\mathcal Q$ requires correction with an independently measured
$G(2\Omega)/G(\Omega)$ (\S\ref{sec:transduction-methods}). }%DIFDELCMD < \\[4pt]
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelFL{\textbf{Frequency selectivity.} The beat response peaks at the region's resonant
rhythm and grows as $1/\gamma$ near its Hopf; state changes can alter both the
generated drive and the recurrent gain \cite{ruffini2025cfc}.
}%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{The quantitative gain--bandwidth relation is not tested here in biological data.
}%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelendFL Sweep \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$\Delta f$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{the beat }\DIFaddendFL through the intrinsic band across
\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{brain statesand track the
}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{states, measuring }\DIFaddendFL peak frequency, height, and width. \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{A matched direct tACS
source tests the shared amplifier. }\DIFaddendFL \\[4pt]
\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\textbf{Timing versus rate.} Beat modulation can exceed the mean shift; their ratio
peaks and then falls as the amplified oscillation rectifies
(\S\ref{sec:timing}--\ref{sec:qif}).
}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{Population modulation and mean response }\DIFaddendFL & \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{Qualitatively consistent with TI altering spike timing more than mean rate in
primates \cite{vieira2024}.
}%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelendFL Compare \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{beat }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{rate }\DIFaddendFL lock-in \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{with mean-rate change in commensurate effect measures,
retaining the finite-amplitude qualification. }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{and mean shift
in the same units. Synaptic blockade tests recurrent amplification alongside
an independent cellular mixing measurement. Distinguish rate modulation from
single-neuron phase locking. }\DIFaddendFL \\[4pt]
\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\textbf{Network readout.} Population nonlinearity and synaptic recurrence contribute
to the measured response.
}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{Steady versus beat-dependent effects }\DIFaddendFL & \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{Most pyramidal TI responses vanish under synaptic blockade \cite{caldas2024},
supporting network involvement without excluding cellular mixing.
}%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{Test whether synaptic blockade reduces recurrent amplification while preserving
a cellular detector. }%DIFDELCMD < \\[4pt]
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelFL{\textbf{Equal-power carrier control.} At second order, equal local RMS
polarization gives the same steady source when the carriers see the same DC
coefficient. TI also supplies a beat; either waveform can induce an oscillation
if the mean shift crosses a bifurcation.
}%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{Demonstrated in the PING mean field (Fig.~\ref{fig:qif_regimes}c,d).
The corresponding spiking-network and in vivo controls are not provided here.
}%DIFDELCMD < & %%%
\DIFdelFL{Compare two tones with one }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{Compare TI with an unmodulated }\DIFaddendFL carrier
of matched \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{RMS polarization }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{local RMS input }\DIFaddendFL and duration, checking the DC coefficient across
the carrier interval. Measure mean activity, intrinsic oscillations, and \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{the
}\DIFaddendFL beat \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{lock-in separately to distinguish the
steady
shift from the beat response}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{separately}\DIFaddendFL . \\
\DIFdelbeginFL %DIFDELCMD < \end{longtable}
%DIFDELCMD < \endgroup
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{\DIFaddFL{Tests separating the proposed mechanisms. The detailed evidence matrix
and qualifications are in the supplement (\S\ref{supp:prediction-evidence}).
RMS matching refers to the detector input, not electrode or tissue power.}}
\label{tab:predictions}
\end{table}
\DIFaddend

\DIFaddbegin \FloatBarrier
\DIFaddend \section{Conclusion}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{For weak perturbations of ongoing activity, a nonzero second-order response makes
the TI beat proportional to the product of the local carrier polarizations,
rather than the envelope minimum. Its steady source follows }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Access, detection, and amplification determine different parts of the TI response. With nonzero curvature, a weak static
scalar detector generates a beat weighted by the carrier-polarization product
and a steady source weighted by }\DIFaddend their summed squares\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{;
the magnitude of this source and the ideal envelope mean have no strict interior
maximum in a compartment uniform in conductivity, access, and detection. Fast access through nodes or the AIS is represented by a short effective membrane time, with periaxonal circuitry
motivating distinct axonal and somatic responses. Its TI transfer and the absolute
response at human field strengths require calibration.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. The steady sign depends on the detector: static JR
curvature and fast-carrier QIF dynamics can give opposite signs. At fixed
field shapes and neural state, current-ratio changes rescale the quadratic beat map; the opposite
envelope and mean-square centroid shifts are specific to the head-model example.
With homogeneous conductivity and uniform scalar coupling and detection,
the steady-source magnitude has no strict interior maximum.
}\DIFaddend

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{TI and direct low-frequency stimulation share the circuit amplifier when the generated input, observable, and operating state are matched and both responses
are linear. Resonance alone then gives TI no efficiency advantage; finite-amplitude
responses near the Hopf can depart from that ratio.
Fast population relaxation
recovers a static rate map with dynamic synapses,
but static carrier mixing
requires relaxation faster than the carrier as well.
Fast access and slow network
resonance can coexist, with each filter counted once; a large response alone
identifies neither an efficient detector nor its microscopic mechanism. }%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Near a Hopf bifurcation, gain grows inversely with modal damping and is shared
with matched direct tACS. }\DIFaddend In the pinned JR \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{sweep, beat-locked rate modulationgrows more than the mean-rate
shift toward the Hopf. Their ratio peaks and then falls as the amplified response
itself rectifies, so timing can dominate without }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{column it initially
favors beat modulation, while rectification of the amplified oscillation
increasingly contributes to }\DIFaddend the mean shift\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{vanishing.
}%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{At larger fields, the rectified shift can carry the QIF circuit across its Hopf
and induce gamma. An equal-power unmodulated carrier produces the same transition
in the mean-field control, while TI adds the beat response. Separately measuring
local polarization, the generated beat , and circuit gain distinguishes these mechanismsand calibrates the three stages}\DIFdelend .
\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{At stronger drive, a steady shift alone can induce gamma, including under an
equal-RMS unmodulated carrier in the mean field. Physiological kHz access remains
to be calibrated. Local-polarization measurements, independent-amplitude tests,
beat sweeps across states, and equal-RMS controls distinguish these mechanisms.
}\DIFaddend

\section*{Code and data availability}
Simulation code, cached results, and figure-generation scripts are provided at
\href{https://github.com/giulioruffini/TI-demodulation-paper}{the paper's GitHub repository};
the public archive is linked through
\href{https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.21009618}{Zenodo (doi:10.5281/zenodo.21009618)}.
The repository \texttt{README} maps figures to their scripts and distinguishes
recomputation from replotting cached data. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{A reconstructed producer with explicit
run settings reproduces every archived spectral bin of the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The }\DIFaddend two-tone kHz \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{illustration (Fig.~\ref{fig:khz_twotone}) exactly. The LaNMMsetup
is a drawn schematic. The LaNMM carrier comparison has both measurement-window
caches and a reproducible summary script}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{example and both carrier-measurement windows in the laminar
neural mass model (LaNMM) have cached outputs and reproduction scripts}\DIFaddend . The FEM \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{summary arrays and
plotting
script reproduce }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{producers }\path{code/fem_quadmaps.py} \DIFadd{and
}\path{code/fem_orientation_interface.py} \DIFadd{compute the spatial statistics;
}\path{code/make_fem_fig.py} \DIFadd{redraws }\DIFaddend Fig.~\ref{fig:fem} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{without the head model. Recomputing its maps
from the underlying fields additionally }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{from cached arrays.
Recomputation }\DIFaddend requires Neuroelectrics' nonpublic \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{temporal-interference }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{TI }\DIFaddend library and the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{stored }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{original
}\DIFaddend IXI002 fields\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{and labels;
the input hashes and settings }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, labels, and, for interface checks, mesh and potentials.
The per-node exporter }\path{code/fem_export_nodes.py} \DIFadd{is included;
independent validation and release of its planned FreeSurfer-only data file
remain pending. That export excludes the atlas-label and interface analyses
and will carry a separate CC BY-SA 4.0 license with IXI attribution.
Settings, BLAKE2b-512 input hashes, and reproduction scope }\DIFaddend are recorded in
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\texttt{docs/fem\_ixi002\_violante.md} and \texttt{code/fem\_quadmaps.json}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \path{docs/fem_ixi002_violante.md}\DIFadd{, }\path{code/fem_quadmaps.json}\DIFadd{,
}\path{code/fem_orientation_interface.json}\DIFadd{, and }\path{docs/fem_portable.md}\DIFaddend .
The code in this paper's repository is released under CC-BY-4.0.

\DIFadd{JR and LaNMM voltages are in mV and rates in s$^{-1}$. QIF voltages and current-like
drives are dimensionless model variables; QIF rates are physical rates, integrated
in ms$^{-1}$ and converted to Hz where indicated. The uncoupled MPR derivation explicitly uses membrane-time units; the main
population equations retain physical time and rate}\DIFaddend . All frequencies and time
constants in a formula must use the same time unit. In the FEM comparison $\mathbf n^*$ is the direction maximizing the envelope
at balanced currents, held fixed during the ratio sweep. A star otherwise marks a fixed point or,
on a bifurcation parameter, its critical value. Subscripts E and I identify
excitatory and inhibitory populations; they are not field or current variables.

$\delta\eta_E,\delta\eta_E^*$ & Rectified E-drive shift and its Hopf threshold & normalized & \S\ref{sec:qif-methods}, \S\ref{sec:qif} \\
$P_1,P_2,r_P,\varphi_X$ & Deep/superficial pyramidal populations; JR and LaNMM pyramidal rates & labels; s$^{-1}$ for rates & \S\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\ref{sec:nmm}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\ref{sec:timing}}\DIFaddend ; \S\ref{sec:lanmm-methods} \\
\DIFaddbegin \ifappendices
\DIFaddend $z,\alpha,\mathcal F,\varepsilon_{\rm SL}$ & Stuart--Landau mode, growth rate, forcing and forcing amplitude & normalized mode; s$^{-1}$ for the others & App.~\ref{app:sl} \\
\DIFaddbegin \fi
\DIFaddend \addlinespace
\multicolumn{4}{@{}l}{\textbf{Measurement}}\\*
$L_{\cos},L_{\sin},A_\Omega$ & Signed lock-in quadratures and nonnegative amplitude & measured voltage or rate units & Eq.~\eqref{eq:lockin} \\
$A_{\rm open}$ & Magnitude of the open-loop rate beat & s$^{-1}$ & \S\ref{sec:jcurve} \\
$w_k,t_k,v_k,\bar v$ & Hann weights, sample times, voltage samples and weighted mean & 1; s; mV; mV & Eq.~\eqref{eq:lockin} \\
$A_{\rm test},\theta_0$ & Amplitude and phase of the lock-in test tone & readout units; rad & \S\ref{sec:transduction-methods} \\
$\Delta t,N_{\rm grid}$ & Integration step and number of parameter combinations & s or ms; count & \S\ref{sec:jr-methods} \\
$A_\Omega/\langle r_E\rangle$ & Beat-locked population modulation depth & dimensionless & \S\ref{sec:qif-methods} \\
$\mathrm{PPC}$ & Pairwise phase consistency of single-neuron spike phases & dimensionless & \S\ref{sec:qif-methods} \\
$f_{\rm out},S(f_c)$ & Dominant output frequency; LaNMM mean off-alpha lock-in & Hz; mV & App.~\ref{sec:vocab}; \S\ref{sec:lanmm-methods} \\
\end{longtable}

\DIFaddbegin \renewcommand{\theequation}{B\arabic{equation}}
\renewcommand{\theHequation}{appendixB.\arabic{equation}}
\setcounter{equation}{0}
\clearpage
\section{\DIFadd{Simulated models: equations and parameters}}\label{app:models}
\DIFadd{For a synapse from population $Y$ to $X$, the resulting first-generation neural
mass model (NMM1) equations are
\cite{ruffini2025rosetta}
}\begin{equation}
\DIFadd{\hat L_{X\leftarrow Y}\big[u_{X\leftarrow Y}\big]=C_{X\leftarrow Y}\,r_Y,\qquad
v_X=\Lambda[E]+\!\!\sum_{Y:\,C_{X\leftarrow Y}\neq0}\!\!u_{X\leftarrow Y},\qquad
r_X=\Sig(v_X).
\label{eq:nmm1}
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{The operator $\hat L$ is the inverse synaptic filter, $u_{X\leftarrow Y}$ its
postsynaptic potential, $C_{X\leftarrow Y}$ the connectivity, and $\Lambda[E]$
the direct polarization caused by the applied field $E$ (V/m). The rates $r_X,r_Y$
are in s$^{-1}$, the potentials $v_X,u_{X\leftarrow Y}$ in mV, and the
pathway weights $C_{X\leftarrow Y}$ dimensionless. This field contribution enters
alongside the synaptic potentials, without first passing through a synapse.
}

\subsection*{\DIFadd{Jansen--Rit column}}
\DIFadd{Writing $y_0$ for the excitatory PSP driving the interneurons, $y_1$ and $y_2$ for the
excitatory and inhibitory PSPs onto the pyramidal population, external input $p$, and the
applied-field perturbation $s(t)$ (\S\ref{sec:coupling}) entering the pyramidal sigmoid,
}\begin{align}
\DIFadd{\ddot y_0 }&\DIFadd{= Aa\,\Sig\!\big(y_1-y_2+s(t)\big)-2a\dot y_0-a^2 y_0, \label{eq:jr0}}\\
\DIFadd{\ddot y_1 }&\DIFadd{= Aa\,\big[\,p+C_2\,\Sig(C_1 y_0)\,\big]-2a\dot y_1-a^2 y_1,}\\
\DIFadd{\ddot y_2 }&\DIFadd{= Bb\,C_4\,\Sig(C_3 y_0)-2b\dot y_2-b^2 y_2,
}\end{align}
\DIFadd{The pyramidal output passes through $h_e$ to give $y_0$; the excitatory
and inhibitory interneurons receive $C_1y_0$ and $C_3y_0$, and return through
weights $C_2$ and $C_4$. The excitatory and inhibitory impulse responses are
$h_e(t)=Aa\,t e^{-at}$ and $h_i(t)=Bb\,t e^{-bt}$ for $t\ge0$.
The equations are integrated as six first-order ODEs; the LFP proxy is $v=y_1-y_2$ \cite{jansen1995}.
Table~\ref{tab:params} gives the reference parameters, with excitatory and
inhibitory synaptic decay times of $10$ and $20$~ms. Methods specifies the
external-rate $p$ and connectivity $C$ sweeps used to change the operating state.
}

\begin{table}[h]
\color{blue}\centering\small
\caption{\color{blue}Jansen--Rit parameters \cite{jansen1995}.}
\label{tab:params}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
Symbol & Meaning & Value\\
\midrule
$A,\,B$ & excit./inhib.\ PSP gain & $3.25,\ 22$ mV\\
$a,\,b$ & synaptic rates & $100,\ 50$ s$^{-1}$\\
$v_0,\,e_0,\,\rho$ & sigmoid inflection, max rate$/2$, slope & $6$ mV, $2.5$ s$^{-1}$, $0.56$ mV$^{-1}$\\
$C$ & global connectivity & $135$\\
$C_1,C_2,C_3,C_4$ & couplings & $C,\,0.8C,\,0.25C,\,0.25C$\\
$p$ & external input (bifurcation knob) & $0$--$400$ Hz\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\DIFadd{The alpha limit cycle lies between Hopf points near $p=90$ and
$316$~s$^{-1}$. It coexists with a lower stable fixed point up to the
saddle-node on an invariant circle (SNIC, $p\approx114$), then with a
large-amplitude cycle up to its saddle-node near $p=137$. The SNIC
terminates the large-amplitude branch, not the alpha cycle.
}


\subsection{\DIFadd{Operating-point index and sigmoid curvature}}\label{app:peix}
\DIFadd{Because the static source scales with $\Sig''(v^*)$, its state dependence
can be expressed through the Population Excitation--Inhibition Index (PEIX)
\cite{ruffini2025cfc}, which measures the operating point's signed departure from maximal sigmoid slope:
}\begin{equation}
\DIFadd{\mathrm{PEIX}(v^*)=\operatorname{sign}(v^*-v_0)
\left\{1-\operatorname{sech}^2\!\Big[\tfrac{\rho}{2}\,(v^*-v_0)\Big]\right\}.
\label{eq:peix}
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{It is zero at $v_0$ and approaches $\pm1$ in the saturated tails, with
positive sign above $v_0$. For the logistic,
$\Sig'/\Sig'(v_0)=1-|\mathrm{PEIX}|$, $\Sig'(v_0)=e_0\rho/2$, and
$\Sig''=-\rho\Sig'\operatorname{sign}(\mathrm{PEIX})\sqrt{|\mathrm{PEIX}|}$.
Thus this measurable state index determines the side and saturation, while
mixing follows curvature: zero at the inflection and in the tails, largest
on the flanks.
}

\subsection{\DIFadd{Static rate with dynamic synapses}}
\DIFadd{A second-order synapse with time constant $\tau_s$ obeys
}\begin{equation}
\DIFadd{r(t)\simeq \frac{F(Q(t))}{\tau_m},\qquad
\tau_s^2\ddot r_{\rm syn}+2\tau_s\dot r_{\rm syn}+r_{\rm syn}=r(t),
\label{eq:slow_synapse}
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{where $r_{\rm syn}$ is the filtered rate entering the recurrent drive in $Q$, with the original
coupling normalization retained. This is the slow-synapse limit: shortening the
effective membrane time makes the transfer appear static while the network retains
its synaptic dynamics.
}

\subsection{\DIFadd{Static and dynamic MPR mixing}}\label{app:mpr-coefficients}\label{app:mpr-mixing}
\DIFadd{Here time is measured in units of $\tau_m$, rate in units of $1/\tau_m$, and
$\omega$ is angular frequency in membrane-time units; its physical value is
$\omega/\tau_m$. The steady-rate curve $F$ (\S\ref{sec:dynamic},
}\ifsupp \DIFadd{main-text}\fi\DIFadd{\ Eq.~\eqref{eq:Fmpr}) averages the single-neuron rate
$f(q)=\sqrt q/\pi$ for $q>0$, and zero otherwise, over the excitability
distribution. Here $q=\eta+I$ is the neuron's constant total drive and $\eta$
its excitability. An uncoupled MPR population at drive $Q$ has rate $r^*=F(Q)$.
A weak current $I_{\rm ac}\cos\omega t$ changes its mean rate.
To second order in $I_{\rm ac}$, the carrier-averaged shift of $r$ is $I_{\rm ac}^2\kappa_{\rm DC}(Q,\omega)$, where
}\begin{equation}
\DIFadd{\kappa_{\rm DC}(Q,\omega)=\frac{r^*\,[\omega^2+3d^2-\nu^2]}{(d^2+\nu^2)\{[d^2+\nu^2-\omega^2]^2+4d^2\omega^2\}},
\qquad r^*=F(Q),\quad d=\frac{\Delta}{\pi r^*},\quad \nu=2\pi r^*.
\label{eq:crossover}
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{The physical rate shift is $I_{\rm ac}^2\kappa_{\rm DC}/\tau_m$.
The fixed-point eigenvalues in normalized time are $-d\pm i\nu$: $d$ is the relaxation rate and $\nu$
the ringing angular frequency, both in membrane-time units; their physical
values are $d/\tau_m$ and $\nu/\tau_m$.
}

\DIFadd{To obtain Eq.~\eqref{eq:crossover}, expand about $(r^*,v^*)$, with
$v^*=-d/2$. The Jacobian and unit-current phasor are
}\[
\DIFadd{\mathsf J=\begin{pmatrix}2v^*&2r^*\\-2\pi^2r^*&2v^*\end{pmatrix},\qquad
\mathbf h=(i\omega\mathsf I_2-\mathsf J)^{-1}\binom{0}{1},
}\]
\DIFadd{where $\mathsf I_2$ is the identity matrix and the first-order response is
$I_{\rm ac}\operatorname{Re}(\mathbf h e^{i\omega t})$.
Writing $h_r,h_v$ for its rate and voltage components, with an overbar denoting
complex conjugation and $\operatorname{Re}$ the real part, the carrier-averaged
quadratic source per $I_{\rm ac}^2$ is
}\[
\DIFadd{\mathbf b_2=\binom{\operatorname{Re}(h_r\overline{h_v})}{\big(|h_v|^2-\pi^2|h_r|^2\big)/2}.
}\]
\DIFadd{The mean second-order correction is $-I_{\rm ac}^2\mathsf J^{-1}\mathbf b_2$;
its rate component gives Eq.~\eqref{eq:crossover}.
}

\DIFadd{The low- and high-frequency limits are
}\begin{equation}
\DIFadd{\kappa_{\rm DC}(Q,\omega)\xrightarrow[\ \omega\to0\ ]{}\tfrac14F''(Q),\qquad
\kappa_{\rm DC}(Q,\omega)\xrightarrow[\ \omega\gg d,\nu\ ]{}\frac{F'(Q)}{2\omega^2}.
\label{eq:crossover_limits}
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{In the first limit the population tracks its static curve, whose curvature rectifies
the input. In the second, the voltage excursion is approximately $I_{\rm ac}/\omega$; its
square supplies a slow drive that the population reads through $F'$. Above the
inflection of $F$, these limits have opposite signs and the coefficient vanishes at
$\omega_z=\sqrt{\nu^2-3d^2}$. The $300$~Hz and kHz carriers
are on the fast side for the parameters of the pyramidal--interneuron network gamma
(PING) circuit studied here. That classification is
parameter-dependent: a shorter effective membrane time can bring the same physical
carrier into the adiabatic regime.
}

\fi

\let\ne@stdsection\section
\renewcommand{\section}{\clearpage\ne@stdsection}
\makeatother
\ifappendices
\renewcommand{\thesection}{S\arabic{section}}
\renewcommand{\theHsection}{supp.\arabic{section}}
\setcounter{section}{0}
\renewcommand{\theequation}{S\arabic{equation}}
\renewcommand{\theHequation}{supp.\arabic{equation}}
\setcounter{equation}{0}
\renewcommand{\thefigure}{S\arabic{figure}}
\renewcommand{\theHfigure}{supp.\arabic{figure}}
\setcounter{figure}{0}
\renewcommand{\thetable}{S\arabic{table}}
\renewcommand{\theHtable}{supp.\arabic{table}}
\setcounter{table}{0}
\section{\DIFadd{Carrier access}}\label{supp:access}
\DIFadd{These calculations support the distinction between applied field and local
polarization in \S\ref{sec:khz} and its kHz demonstrations.
}\subsection{\DIFadd{Compartment access and the passive-cable estimate}}\label{app:access}
\DIFadd{The passive compartment equation $\mathsf C_m\dot{\mathbf V}+\mathsf G_m\mathbf V=\mathbf I^0-\mathbf p_EE$
\cite{ruffini2021nmm2} gives the voltage perturbation
$\delta\mathbf V(\omega)=-(\mathsf G_m+i\omega\mathsf C_m)^{-1}\mathbf p_EE(\omega)$. In SI units, $\mathbf V$ contains the compartment membrane potentials (V), $\mathsf C_m$ and
$\mathsf G_m$ are the capacitance (F) and conductance (S) matrices, $\mathbf I^0$ is the
endogenous current (A), and $\mathbf p_E$ is the field-to-current coupling
(A per V/m).
}

\DIFadd{Geometry can also alter the relevant response mode. For a passive cable, the
magnitude of the frequency-dependent space constant is
}\begin{equation}
\DIFadd{\ell(\omega)=\ell_0(1+\omega^2\tau_m^2)^{-1/4},
\label{eq:lambda}
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{where $\ell_0$ is its static value. At $1$~kHz and $\tau_m=16$~ms, this length
is about one tenth of its static value, so compartments that are strongly coupled
at low frequency can respond differently to the carrier. A somatic charging time
alone therefore does not determine every extracellular-field response mode.
For a sufficiently long passive cable with a sealed end in a uniform axial field,
the end polarization is proportional to this space constant. Each carrier then
acquires the factor $(1+\omega^2\tau_m^2)^{-1/4}$, so the quadratic beat is
attenuated by $(1+\omega_c^2\tau_m^2)^{-1/2}$, about $10^{-2}$ at $1$~kHz and
$10^{-3}$ at $10$~kHz for $16$~ms. These differ from the point-membrane estimates;
soma loading and finite cable length further change the transfer.
Thus cable geometry can improve carrier access without changing the somatic
membrane time.
}

\subsection{\DIFadd{Measured responses and candidate access sites}}
\DIFadd{Somatic passive time constants are commonly $10$--$30$~ms; reported human pyramidal
values include approximately $12$~ms \cite{deitcher2017} and $16.5\pm3.7$~ms
\cite{eyal2016}. For the illustrative $\tau_m=16$~ms used here, the power factor
$|H_m|^2$ is about $10^{-4}$ at $1$~kHz, $2.5\times10^{-5}$ at $2$~kHz, and
$10^{-6}$ at $10$~kHz. A static nonlinearity placed after this point-membrane response
therefore receives strongly attenuated carriers.
}

\DIFadd{Cohen et al.'s periaxonal circuit provides a reason to distinguish axonal from
somatic access \cite{cohen2020periaxonal}. A conducting submyelin space, about
$12$~nm wide and partly sealed at the paranodes, separates the voltage across
myelin from that across the axonal membrane. Their minimum fitted axonal decay
was approximately $1$~ms. This is a response to injected current in a distributed
cable, rather than a measured transfer from a TI field. The AIS is another candidate
site, with a high sodium-channel density \cite{kole2008ais}; this property supports
nonlinear sensitivity but does not fix its charging time.
}

\ifjne
\subsection{\DIFadd{Access assumptions in related models}}
\DIFadd{Short access times motivate the model but remain preparation-dependent.
The $1$ and $0.4$~ms cases of \cite{esmaeilpour2021} precede our effective-time
hypothesis. Luff et al.'s pulse-width-modulated TI model uses $1$~ms within a
$1$--$100$~ms passive-time sweep \cite{luff2024}. The $0.7~\mu$s charging
measured by Semenov et al.\ under strong microsecond field pulses
\cite{semenov2025} concerns a different response mode from intracellular
current charging or periaxonal conduction. A $10$--$100$-fold reduction in
QIF membrane time is therefore a sensitivity hypothesis, whose population
consequence follows from the slow-synapse limit.
}

\fi

demodulated response (normalized to the $\tau\!\to\!0$ value) vs.\ carrier
frequency up to $10$~kHz. With direct coupling ($\tau\!\to\!0$) the response is
flat---carrier-independent. A slow somatic membrane ($\tau_m\!\approx\!16$~ms)
rolls off as $1/f_c^2$ (dashed guide); the assumed fast response
($\tau=0.2$~ms) retains about $0.14$ of the direct response at $2$~kHz and $0.04$
at $3.9$~kHz. These are sensitivity cases, not measurements of anatomical
compartments.}}

\end{figure}

\setcounter{figure}{5}

Two equal tones at $f_1=1994.543$ and $f_2=2005.457$~Hz, beating at
$10.914$~Hz, pass through the assumed low-pass ($\tau=0.2$~ms) into the recurrent column.
\emph{(a)} The input amplitude spectrum has lines only at $f_1$ and $f_2$; in the

\begin{figure}[t!]

\caption{\DIFaddFL{\textbf{Demodulation at a kHz carrier (Jansen--Rit, closed loop).}
The applied field passes through an explicit membrane filter before the sigmoid.
\emph{(a)} Closed-loop demodulated $A_\Omega$ at $\Delta f=f_0$ vs.\ carrier $f_c$ for
three access models: direct ($\tau\!\to\!0$, carrier-independent), fast
($\tau=0.2$~ms), and somatic ($\tau_m=16$~ms; $1/f_c^2$ roll-off).
The fast time is assumed rather than assigned to a measured compartment.
\emph{(b)} Output spectrum for a
$2$~kHz carrier delivered through the fast element: an alpha line at $f_0$,
synthesized by the column although the input carries no alpha power.}}
